The shipped default is **1:1, no reduction**, which is a fab's decision to make and not a defect, so the test asserts it: a stencil cut to pad size deposits as much solder as the pad can hold, which is how an 0402 tombstones, and a project that ships that silently is different from one that ships it on purpose.
- DONE: **the pick-and-place file put bottom-side parts on the top of the board.** `export_cpl` decided the face by looking at the first pad of the footprint, so a part the design places on the bottom - the `Side` component, which the KiCad importer reads from `(layer "B.Cu")` and sync stores once so every consumer agrees - came out as `Top` whenever it shared a footprint with top-side parts. Every one of them would have been assembled on the wrong face. It reads `Side` now and falls back to the pad guess only when nothing states one. Proven both ways: `R2,30.000mm,20.000mm,Top,90` on the previous commit against `Bottom` on this one, with position and rotation asserted alongside.
- This is the same shape as `CourtyardClearanceRule` filtering for a `layer_mask` no builder sets, and as the DSL writer and the Gerber exporter each joining a net's branches: a fact is established in one place and a consumer somewhere else quietly guesses instead. Three of the four came out of testing the chain rather than the links.
- DONE: **the bill of materials is checked, and it was right.** A purchaser orders from that file: a part missing from it does not get bought and the board cannot be built, a part counted twice is money spent on a component nobody needs. The check is on what is accounted for rather than on line count, because grouping is the point of the file - two identical resistors are one line of quantity two, and the capacitor beside them is not. Values survive as the source states them. Mutation-checked by dropping one component from `group_components`, which turns it red.
- DONE: **the silkscreen drew parts assembled underneath onto the top legend.** `export_silkscreen` decided which legend a part belongs on by asking whether its footprint has pads on that layer, so a bottom-side part sharing a footprint with top-side ones was printed on both the wrong face and the right one. It reads the `Side` component now, exactly as the pick-and-place file does since the previous fire, and falls back to the pad question only when nothing states a side. The test fails on the previous commit: `the top legend must not draw around a part assembled underneath`.
- ~~**Recorded as a gap rather than tested for: the legend has no text.**~~ **Closed, and it stayed written here for a fire after it was.** A stroke font landed in `cypcb-export` and the legend has carried designators since; the module header kept saying "Designators rendered as simple crosshair marks (not full text)" the whole time, so the code and this file agreed on something neither of them was still doing. Both corrected on 2026-08-07, along with the defect the printing had introduced - see the end of this vector.
- **This vector is finished.** Eight of the thirteen files `export` writes are now read back against the design that produced them - copper on both layers, drill, outline, soldermask, paste stencil, pick-and-place, bill of materials, silkscreen. Five defects came out of it, and four share one shape: a fact is established in one place and a consumer somewhere else quietly guesses. Not one of them was visible to a test that stopped at the model.
- DONE: **the gate lints test code now, and turning the flag on found another test that could not fail.** `clippy --all-targets` was never part of stage 2, which is how a tautological assertion in `cypcb-rules` survived long enough to guard a constant that was wrong by four fields. Clearing the workspace for it turned up the same shape in `cypcb-lsp`: `assert!(MAX_DIAGNOSTICS >= 50)` and `<= 200`, two literals compared. It is a behaviour test now - a board of twenty overlapping parts floods the checker, and the list has to come back capped at 100 with a final entry saying how many were dropped. **The first version of that test passed while checking nothing**, because a file of broken syntax collapses into one parse error; making the assertion unconditional exposed it, and the flood comes from DRC violations instead. The rest were mechanical: 8 `unused_mut` and 6 `len() == 0`, fixed with `clippy --fix`.
- **Gate status, measured 2026-08-06 after the lint change: all eight stages pass.** `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` -> `=== All stages passed ===`, with clippy now covering test code. The browser dependencies are still in the container, so stage 6 ran.
- DONE: **running it found two more tests that were not doing their job, and neither was visible to `cargo test`.** Stage 7 runs `benchmark_validation` with `--ignored`, which the normal test run skips, so 61 green targets sat beside a red gate. Its per-fixture DRC ratchets still held the pre-clearance-fix numbers - 0, 167, 110 against a measured 1, 186, 146 - and `benchmark_full_matrix` looked up strategies by the names `PathFinder` and `ImprovedAStar` while the field holds `StrategyKind`'s `Display` output, `pathfinder` and `improved-astar`: both lookups found nothing and the test died on `expect` before reaching the comparison it exists for. Ratchets re-measured with the reason written at the table, lookups matched case-insensitively and given a panic message that prints what the table actually holds.
- DONE: **the gate had been red at stage 1 for six fires while every one of them reported green.** `cargo fmt --check` is the first thing `scripts/quality-gate.sh` runs and the first thing I stopped running: each fire verified with `cargo test` and `cargo clippy`, which is not the same claim. 33 files were unformatted, in code from the parser work onward - `cargo fmt` moved 337 lines and deleted 168. Not one of them is a behaviour change; the suite is byte-identical in result before and after.
- **One file is exempt and says why.** rustfmt puts every coordinate of the stroke font on its own line, turning a 130-line table into 400 lines nobody can proofread. A glyph is a drawing held as a table and has to be read across one line, so `silk_text::glyph` carries `#[rustfmt::skip]` with that reason written above it. Its previous home in `cypcb-export` had the same shape and no attribute, which is part of why stage 1 went red and stayed there.
- **Gate status, measured 2026-08-08, all eight stages:** `=== All stages passed ===`. Stage 6 rebuilds `viewer/pkg` first, which is the change from the previous fire being exercised for the first time - the browser suite now tests the engine in this tree rather than whatever was last compiled.
- NEXT-ACTION: none in this vector. Worth remembering what it cost twice: a suite that passes is not a gate that passes. The first time the difference was two stages nobody ran; the second time it was one stage nobody re-ran after changing the code it checks.
- DONE: **`cypcb route` sent people to a download page for a board it could already route.** The command needs a Java runtime and a `freerouting.jar` the project does not ship, and missing either ended the run - "FreeRouting JAR not found" or "Java not found. Please install Java 21+" - with a GitHub release link and no mention of `--in-house`, the router compiled into the same binary that needs neither. Both dead ends now open with a command the reader can paste. Measured on this container, where the jar is a local symlink and there is no Java: `cypcb route examples/blink.cypcb` prints `Java not found, and FreeRouting is a Java program.` then `No jar and no Java needed - this binary has its own autorouter:` and `cypcb route examples/blink.cypcb --in-house`; run from `/tmp` with no jar in reach the same offer follows `FreeRouting JAR not found.`. The offer is real: that exact command writes 52 segments and 4 vias in 0.06s and exits 0.
- **The tracker's own next-action for this was half the defect.** It named the missing jar; the missing runtime is the one this container actually hits, and it lived in `cypcb-router` as a library error the CLI passed straight through. A library has no business naming CLI flags, so the CLI matches `RoutingError::JavaNotFound` and writes the sentence itself. One test walks all three dead ends and asserts each offers a pasteable command: `every_freerouting_dead_end_names_the_router_that_needs_nothing`.
- **That next-action was written from this file rather than from the code, and it was already done.** "The silkscreen has no text" pointed at a gap `crates/cypcb-export/src/gerber/font.rs` had already closed - a stroke font, 36 glyphs, printing every part's name. Reading the module before planning around it is what turned up the defect below. The old gap line further up this vector is struck through.
- DONE: **the legend printed part names the checker could not see, and half of them landed on copper.** The font lived inside `cypcb-export`, so `silk-clearance` measured courtyard outlines while the exporter drew designators the rule had never heard of - the same shape as every other defect in this vector: a fact established in one place, a consumer somewhere else quietly guessing. Ink on a pad resists solder, so the joint under it is starved or open. The layout moved to `cypcb_world::silk_text` and both crates read it; the checker went from **2 silk violations across the example boards to 50**, on 12 boards where it had found them on one. Every one of them was real: on an 0805 the name was printed from 0.6mm to 1.6mm above the part's origin while the pads reach 0.725mm, so the ink crossed the part's own copper by 0.125mm.
- DONE: **and the names moved off the copper.** `designator_strokes` takes the height of the part's own artwork now - `artwork_rise`, the courtyard or the footprint's own lines, rotated with the part - and sits the name two stroke widths above it rather than at a fixed fraction of the text height. **50 violations down to 12**, and the 12 are neighbours: parts placed close enough that one part's name reaches the next part's pads, which is a fault in those boards rather than in the layout.
- **Two of the twelve are neither, and they name the next defect.** `C1 silkscreen over C1.2` on `alignment-test.cypcb` and `LED_STAT_D1 silkscreen over LED_STAT_D1.2` on `v2-modules.cypcb` are parts whose own name still lands on their own pads, and the reason is `chip_footprint` in `crates/cypcb-world/src/footprint/smd.rs`: **the courtyard is built from the body, not from the land pattern.** An 0805 gets a 2.5 x 1.75mm courtyard around a 2.0 x 1.25mm body while its pads span 2.9mm - they stick out 0.2mm each side. Rotate the part 90 degrees and that overhang is above the origin, past the courtyard the name was placed to clear. IPC-7351 puts the courtyard around the land pattern plus a margin; this library puts it around the body. `CourtyardClearanceRule` is measuring the same wrong box.
- DONE: **eight of the thirteen built-in footprints had copper outside their own courtyard.** Each builder derived its courtyard from the body and none of them looked at the land pattern, so the rectangle that says "this is the room the part needs" was narrower than the part's own pads. Measured over `FootprintLibrary::new()`, worst overhang per footprint, in nanometres:

| footprint | copper outside the courtyard, before | after |
|---|---|---|
| TQFP-32 | 1 500 000 | 0 |
| SOIC-8, SOIC-14 | 700 000 | 0 |
| 2512 | 550 000 | 0 |
| 1206 | 425 000 | 0 |
| 0603, 0805 | 200 000 | 0 |
| 0402 | 50 000 | 0 |
| the other five | 0 | 0 |

- `Footprint::with_ipc_courtyard` builds it from the union of body and pads plus `IPC_COURTYARD_EXCESS` (0.25mm per side), and all seven construction sites call it - there is one courtyard formula in the project now instead of seven, two of which were bare literals. The 0805 goes from 2.5 x 1.75mm to **3.4 x 1.95mm**, which is 2.9mm of land pattern plus the excess.
- **Both consumers of that rectangle were under-reporting, and they moved in opposite directions.** `courtyard-clearance` across the examples went **1 -> 3**: with an honest box, `v2-modules.cypcb` shows `PSU_U1 ↔ PSU_C_IN` and `PSU_U1 ↔ PSU_C_OUT` at 0.20mm where assembly wants 0.25mm - two placement collisions that were invisible. `silk-clearance` went **12 -> 8**, because a designator is placed above the courtyard and the courtyard now really does contain the pads. The two self-violations recorded in the previous fire - a part's own name on its own pad - are gone; all eight that remain are neighbours.
- **One existing test asserted the defect.** `test_courtyard_includes_margin` demanded `courtyard == body + 0.5mm` exactly, which is what the builder did and what kept SOIC-8's pads 0.7mm outside their own courtyard. Rewritten to assert the property IPC states - the excess clear of every pad - rather than the arithmetic the old code happened to perform. `crates/cypcb-world/tests/the_courtyard_encloses_the_land_pattern.rs` holds the same line for every footprint in the library, so one added tomorrow cannot bring this back.
- **That next-action rested on a premise that measuring killed.** "Move the parts apart" cannot work: an instance-prefixed name like `DIV_A_RTOP` prints **7.89mm wide** at the 1mm text height, over a 1mm part. No spacing a demo board can adopt clears a label eight times the length of the thing it labels.
- DONE: **the legend is clipped off solderable copper, the way a board house does it.** A fab clips silkscreen before it prints, so a file that needs clipping is a file whose legend nobody has seen - what arrives is not what was sent. `cypcb_world::silk_text::clip_strokes` cuts every stroke where it enters a pad's keepout, `pad_keepouts` builds those squares from the copper on the face being printed, and the exporter and `silk-clearance` both call them. Measured independently of both, by reading the exported `F_Cu.gbr` and `F_SilkS.gbr` and asking whether any silk vertex sits within a pad's required clearance:

| board | ink on copper before | after | silk vertices before | after |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| v2-imports | 10 | 0 | 363 | 383 |
| v2-modules | 13 | 0 | 381 | 395 |
| blink | 0 | 0 | 171 | 207 |
| alignment-test | 0 | 0 | 151 | 183 |

- Across the examples `silk-clearance` goes **8 -> 0** and `courtyard-clearance` stays at 3, which is right: clipping moves ink, not parts. The vertex counts rise because clipping cuts polylines into pieces and because the courtyard outline is now rotated with its part - it was drawn axis-aligned at any angle, with a `TODO` where the rotation should have been, while the rule that measures it had rotated the same corners all along.
- **The clipping is done at the fabricator's clearance, not at a number this crate picked.** `ExportPreset` carries `silk_clearance` - 0.127mm for JLCPCB, 0.2mm for PCBWay - and `SilkConfig` takes it. That keeps `silk-clearance` a real check rather than a formality: a legend clipped for one house and checked against a stricter one still reports. Measured on a board whose name is long enough that its strokes are cut rather than swallowed: `cypcb check --preset jlcpcb` finds 0, `--preset pcbway` finds 1.
- **A name the pads ate is reported, because the file gives no sign of it.** `export_silkscreen_reporting` returns the designators that lost more than half their strokes, and `cypcb export` prints them: `C1234567 is unreadable on the top legend: 24 of its 42 strokes were clipped off copper. Move the part, shorten its name, or place its designator by hand.` Clipping without that warning would trade ink on a joint for a part nobody can identify.
- **Three tests asserted the old behaviour and were rewritten rather than deleted.** Two in the rule and one end to end demanded that ink on a neighbour's pad be *reported*; it is *clipped* now, so they assert the new guarantee - quiet at the clearance the board was clipped for, loud at a stricter one, and no vertex inside a keepout in the file itself.
- DONE: **every Gerber this project ever wrote contradicted its own header, and now none do.** The file declared `%FSLAX26Y26*%` - two integer digits, six decimal ones, the point implied, which is what the `26` means - and then wrote `X3.730000`. A reader that believes the declaration and a reader that believes the data are a thousand apart. Fixed in `nm_to_gerber`, which now emits `X3730000`, with leading zeros suppressed as the `L` declares (`0.5mm` is `500000`, zero is `0`).
- **The fix is per-number, not per-format, and getting that wrong would have been worse than the bug.** Two other kinds of number go through the same conversion and both must *keep* their point: a Gerber aperture definition (`%ADD10C,1.700000*%`) is a size in the file's units that nothing declared a scale for, and an Excellon body whose header is `METRIC,TZ` names no digit count at all. Written the Gerber way they become a 1.7-metre aperture and a hole at 3730mm. `nm_to_decimal` is now the conversion for those eleven call sites; `nm_to_gerber` is for coordinate data only.
- **The leading-zero half of it was caught by a test rather than by me.** The first version wrote `X0000000` for the origin, which keeps six leading zeros under a declaration that says they are suppressed. `the_fabricator_cuts_the_board_the_source_declared` failed on it.
- **Nothing caught the original because every test read the files the way the exporter wrote them.** Ten assertions called `parse::<f64>()` on the digits and took millimetres out, which is only correct if you already assume the bug; four more matched `top.contains("X8.")`, a prefix test that also matches 8.9mm and misses 8mm exactly. They now decode from each file's own `%FS` line, or ask a distance instead of a prefix.
- `crates/cypcb-export/tests/every_number_agrees_with_the_header_above_it.rs` locks all three rules, and **both directions were proven to bite**: reverting the writer fails `no_gerber_coordinate_carries_a_decimal_point`, and pointing apertures back at `nm_to_gerber` fails `an_aperture_definition_keeps_its_decimal_point`, each with the other two staying green.
- **That NEXT-ACTION was wrong when it was written.** `docs/routing.md:96` says a via already "marks the cells its ring covers - drill plus annular", and the ring was measured as an instrument and dropped (line 369: stm32_breakout 124 -> 154). The router's search sees the via ring. Read the file before writing an action from memory - this is the second time that exact mistake is in this tracker.
- DONE: **a mounting hole came back plated, and the fabricator was never told otherwise.** KiCad writes one as `np_thru_hole`; the importer folded it into `is_through_hole` with an ordinary pin, every drill hit was `DrillType::Plated` under the comment "Component pads are always plated", and the NPTH file that every preset names - `-NPTH.drl` for JLCPCB, `_npth.xln` for PCBWay - was never written by the job. A plated M3 hole comes back about a tenth of a millimetre narrower than the screw it was drilled for and shorted to any copper it passes.
- **A hole with no copper is what non-plated means, so no flag was added.** `PadDef::is_non_plated()` reads the geometry - drilled, and no copper on any layer - and the importer drops the copper layers off an `np_thru_hole` pad. That matters because pcbnew writes `(layers "*.Cu" "*.Mask")` on a stock `MountingHole_3.2mm_M3`, so the file's layer list contradicts its own pad type and trusting the layers is the other way to get this wrong.
- **The router had to be told separately, and this is the part that would have shipped a new defect.** `grid.rs` marks obstacles by walking `pad.layers`; a pad with no copper marked nothing, so the search would have laid traces straight across a 3.2mm hole on both layers. A non-plated pad now blocks every copper layer with `CELL_OBSTACLE` rather than `CELL_PAD` - there is no net that belongs in a mounting hole.
- **The drill file's header described a format it was not using.** `; FORMAT={2:4/...}` was hard-coded while the body was written with six decimals - the same defect as the Gerber one, one file over. It states `format.integer_places` and `format.decimal_places` now.
- `crates/cypcb-autoroute/tests/a_mounting_hole_must_come_back_unplated.rs` asks four questions of one imported board, and **each half of the fix was proven necessary**: reverting the importer fails three of them and leaves the router test passing (it has copper to mark), reverting only the grid fails the router test and nothing else. A fifth test runs the real `run_export` and finds the file under the preset's own `drill_npth` name.
- The five benchmark ratchets did not move: no fixture has a mounting hole, so the grid change is inert on them. Gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **a board written in this project's own language can now be screwed to something.** `docs/SYNTAX.md` offered `keepout mounting_hole`, a region the router avoids and nothing that gets drilled, so only an imported KiCad board could carry a real hole.
- **It needed no new syntax and no new component kind.** A mounting hole is a footprint with one drilled pad and no copper, which is what it is and how KiCad carries them too, so four built-ins - `MOUNT-M2`, `MOUNT-M2.5`, `MOUNT-M3`, `MOUNT-M4` - are enough and the existing `component` declaration places them. Drilled to the clearance hole for the screw, not to the screw: 2.2, 2.7, 3.2 and 4.3mm. A `ComponentKind::MountingHole` was considered and dropped - nothing enforces the refdes prefix, so it would have been two enums changed for cosmetics.
- **Two files were asking people to do impossible things, and that is what the feature exposed.** The bill of materials listed every component, so a hole became a line somebody is asked to buy; the placement file listed every component, so a machine was told to place one. `Footprint::is_mechanical()` - no copper anywhere - now excludes them from both. Same shape of answer as `is_non_plated`: the geometry already knows, so nothing has to be remembered.
- `crates/cypcb-cli/tests/a_board_can_be_screwed_to_something.rs` runs a `.cypcb` board through the real `run_export` and checks the hole in every file it touches: in the NPTH drill file and not the PTH one, no copper flash at it, and absent from both assembly files. **The exclusion was proven load-bearing** - stubbing `is_mechanical` to `false` fails `nobody_is_asked_to_buy_a_hole_or_to_place_one` and leaves the other two passing.
- Workspace 101 suites ok, gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **nothing reported copper drawn across a mounting hole.** Every rule that walks pad copper walks past a hole that has none, and the courtyard rule stops a *part* being placed on one, not a *trace* being drawn across one. The router refuses since two fires ago, but the router is not the only way copper reaches a board - a hand-drawn trace, a KiCad import and a poured plane all arrive without its opinion. Measured before writing anything: the default rule set returned **zero** violations about the hole for a trace straight through a 3.2mm one.
- `MountingHoleClearanceRule` measures with `min_edge_clearance`, because that is what such a hole is - a board edge cut into the middle of the board. The same drill exposes the same copper for the same reason, and that number is already in the rule set; a second one would have had to be invented, and no board house published it here.
- **Registered, not just written.** A test runs `run_drc` - what `cypcb check` and the viewer call - and fails with the registration line deleted. Boundary measured rather than assumed: at 0.4mm of clearance quiet, at 0.2mm loud, with the pivot arithmetic written out. The first version of that test had both cases on the same side of the pivot and read the rule as wrong when the arithmetic was.
- **The measurement also caught two false positives the mounting-hole feature had just introduced.** Every hole on every board was reported as an unconnected pin - it has no pin - and as a 0.000mm annular ring against a 0.150mm minimum - it has no copper to ring. Both rules now skip non-plated pads, and a test asserts a board whose only feature is a correctly placed mounting hole is completely clean. Adding a feature without running the checker over it is how a checker starts crying about correct boards.
- Workspace 102 suites ok, gate green 8/8, ratchets unmoved.
- DONE: **`examples/panel-mount.cypcb`** - a 40 x 30mm status board with four M3 holes 4mm in from the corners, which is the board the project could not describe until two fires ago. Proven through the release CLI rather than through a test harness: `cypcb check` clean, `cypcb route --in-house` gives `0 violations` in 9 segments and no vias, and `cypcb export --preset jlcpcb` writes 14 files whose NPTH drill carries exactly `T1C3.200000` and the four corners, the PTH drill carries four hits and none of them at a corner, and the BOM and CPL mention `MOUNT`, `H1`..`H4` zero times.
- **Laying the example out was itself the measurement.** The first arrangement routed with 3 clearance violations and the second with 2; rotating C1 and lifting it 1mm gave 0. An example board that ships with violations teaches the violations.
- **Adding it broke `every_example_reaches_the_files` in two places, and both were assumptions older than mounting holes.** The drill check took the first `.drl` it found - which sorts to `-NPTH.drl` - and then reported every plated pin as missing; it now reads both files, holds each hole against the one it belongs in, and fails a hole that appears in both. The placement check expected every part on the board to have a CPL row; it now excludes mechanical footprints, which is what the exporter does on purpose.
- A test reads `examples/panel-mount.cypcb` from disk and asserts the four holes survive, so the example cannot quietly lose the thing it demonstrates.
- Workspace 102 suites ok, gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **the viewer drew nothing where a mounting hole is.** Every drawing decision starts from the copper - `getPadColor` returns null for a pad on neither copper layer and `drawPad` returned with it - so a board carried four 3.2mm holes through the language, the drill file, the router and the checker, and the screen showed bare laminate at each one. Measured before touching anything: drawing an M3 hole recorded **zero canvas calls**, an empty list.
- A pad with a drill and no copper is drawn as the hole it is: filled in the drill colour at its drilled size, outlined rather than given the brass ring a plated hole gets, because this one is bare. Drawn whichever copper layers are shown, since it is on none of them - that is the view somebody checks mechanical fit in.
- Four tests, one of them the half that must not change: a plated pin still draws its copper and its hole. `drawPad` had to be exported to be tested, the same way `drawKeepout` already was.
- **Recorded, not built: the 3D view does not show any pad's drill.** It builds drilled cylinders for vias only, so a mounting hole is invisible there - but so is every plated pin's hole, which makes it an old gap rather than something this work broke. Worth a fire of its own.
- Viewer suite 226 tests in 26 files, `tsc --noEmit` clean, gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **the 3D view drilled vias and nothing else, so every through-hole board came out solid.** A connector's pins showed copper on the top face and on the bottom face with no hole between them, and a mounting hole - which has no copper - showed nothing at all, on the one view whose job is telling you whether the board fits its enclosure.
- The decision is a pure function, `padDrills(components)`, so what gets drilled and where can be checked without a WebGL context: five vitest cases covering a drilled header, a surface-mount part that must stay undrilled, a mounting hole, per-pad diameters, and a rotated part - a hole placed at the untouched pad offset lands under the neighbouring pin.
- The mesh is a template cylinder scaled per hole, the construction the vias already use, spanning the board thickness plus a hair so the ends are never coplanar with the faces.
- **Proven to reach the scene rather than only the counter**: a playwright case reads `padDrillCount` off the debug surface on blink and fails with the collector stubbed to return nothing - `blink carries through-hole pads and none of them was drilled`. Holes are also held at no more than the pad count, so nothing is drilled twice.
- Gate green 8/8; viewer 231 tests.
- DONE: **the importer read no copper pours at all.** `(zone` appeared nowhere in `pcb_parser.rs` - it dispatched `footprint`, `segment`, `via` and the `gr_*` outline shapes and walked past every pour. A two-layer board with a ground plane, which is close to every two-layer board anybody makes, arrived with no plane: the router treated the poured area as free, the checker measured clearances against copper that was not in the model, and the exported Gerber shipped a board with no ground.
- **What can be carried is carried exactly; the rest is refused by name.** This crate's `Zone` is a rectangle and KiCad's is a polygon, so a rectangular pour comes across as itself and anything else is reported rather than flattened. A bounding box is not a conservative reading of an L-shaped pour - it is copper where the designer deliberately left none, in exactly the places the shape was drawn to avoid.
- Through the release CLI, on a board carrying one rectangular pour and one L-shaped one:

```
"zone_count": 1,
"zone_refusals": [
  "the zone on net GND is a 6-point outline; this importer carries rectangular
   pours only, and a bounding box would put copper where the shape was drawn
   to avoid it"
]
```

  Before this commit neither field existed and both pours vanished without a word.
- A KiCad rule area arrives as a `Keepout` rather than a pour, which matters the other way round: carrying it as a pour would fill the one region on the board that has to stay empty. A pour with no net is refused too - it cannot be filled or checked.
- **Measured and worth recording separately: none of the five benchmark fixtures carries a zone**, so the autorouter has never been measured on a board with a plane. That is a gap in what the numbers in `docs/routing.md` cover, not a defect in this commit.
- Workspace 103 suites ok, gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **`plane_board.kicad_pcb`, the sixth fixture and the first with a ground plane.** A sensor hub - SOIC-14, an LDO, decoupling, pull-ups, three headers - on 50 x 38mm with the bottom layer poured to GND, inset 1mm so the fixture measures routing rather than edge clearance. Generated by `make_plane_board.py`, which knows nothing about the router; `kicad_emit.py` gained a `pour()` that writes rectangles only, because that is what the importer carries exactly.
- Measured, and ratcheted at the measured value plus its own band: **210 routes, 40 introduced violations, 18 shorts, 0 unrouted**. Band from `via_price_sweep::how_much_of_the_price_is_noise` on this board: 33 to 42 across prices 0.22..0.28, 9 wide, and 15 to 20 shorts. Ratchet 49 / 23.
- **25 vias on a 12-part board, and that is the point of the fixture.** Seven surface-mount ground pads sit on F.Cu while the plane is on B.Cu, so each needs a stitching via. No other fixture makes the router do that.
- **What the plane connects, measured rather than assumed: 4 pins, not 11.** The board has eleven ground pins; the pour reaches the four through-hole ones, because a through-hole pin spans the stack and touches the plane where it passes through. The other seven are top-side pads and copper on the top does not touch copper on the bottom. The test was first written expecting eleven - a model where a bottom-side plane silently connects top-side pads would route boards nobody can build.
- Adding a sixth fixture broke two hard-coded counts in `benchmark_parse.rs` (`Expected 5 benchmark fixtures, got 6`), which is the registry doing its job.
- Workspace 104 suites ok, gate green 8/8, and no existing ratchet moved - this adds a board rather than changing one.
- DONE: **the pad-zone-margin table in `docs/routing.md` was measured on two fixtures that were broken at the time, and its conclusion does not survive their repair.** Re-swept on all six boards: `multi_ic` and `qfp_fanout` both moved materially, and `shift_driver` at margin 5 had been carrying `qfp_fanout`'s numbers copied into its column.
- **Read against each board's own measured noise band, almost none of the margin table is signal.** stm32_breakout 23 apart on a band of 30, plane_board 7 on 9, shift_driver and qfp_fanout best at the default. Only `multi_ic` separates - 229 at margin 2 against 318 at the default, 89 apart on a band of 63 - and it wants 2. The published sentence "only the two boards the rule was fitted on want 2" is gone: `qfp_fanout` read 493 against 343 when its headers were stacked and reads 326 against 309 now, well inside its band of 40.
- **Shorts and violation counts point different ways and the doc now separates them.** At the default the two dense boards short far more than at margin 2 - 136 against 86, 177 against 109 - while `qfp_fanout` goes the other way, 147 against 186. A short is a fault a fabricator builds; a 0.05mm clearance miss is usually absorbed.
- The two older three-board tables are kept as history and dated, saying which fixtures they predate and that a number should be re-measured before it is quoted.
- **The constant was not changed.** Moving it invalidates five ratchets and five measured bands, so it is its own piece of work with its own before-and-after rather than a line edited while writing a table.
- DONE: **margin 2 was adopted, measured on all six boards with fresh bands, and reverted.** Totalled it wins - 87 fewer violations and 67 fewer shorts across the set - and it was still the wrong change, for three reasons that only appear when the boards are read one at a time.
- **The gain sits entirely on the two boards the value would have been fitted on.** `stm32_breakout` and `multi_ic` are the pair that wanted 2 in the original three-board sweep. Every fixture added since - `shift_driver` 33 -> 38 shorts, `qfp_fanout` 147 -> 186, `plane_board` 18 -> 24 - is worse at 2, and so is `led_blink`. Four against two, and the two chose the value.
- **`led_blink` gains its first short and it is not a near miss:** `D1 <-> via 'GND': Clearance violation: 0.00mm actual`. A via touching a foreign pad on the simplest board in the project. By this project's own ranking that outweighs violation counts elsewhere.
- **`stm32_breakout` becomes far less predictable**: its spread across via prices 0.22..0.28 widens from 30 violations to 77. A board that moves by 77 under a setting nobody intends to change has a ratchet measuring the weather.
- My own previous NEXT-ACTION here over-claimed - it read the margin table on violation counts alone and said the other four boards paid nothing. They pay in shorts, which this project ranks higher. The before-and-after table is now in `docs/routing.md` and the instrument is in the dropped table, the second one there to be dropped twice.
- All six ratchets hold at the restored default: led_blink 2/0, stm32_breakout 239/136, multi_ic 318/177, shift_driver 81/33, qfp_fanout 309/147, plane_board 40/18, nothing unrouted.
- NEXT-ACTION: none in this vector worth its cost. Seventeen instruments have now been measured and dropped; `docs/routing.md` has said for several fires that the next real gain is a cost model rather than another constant, and two failed attempts at this one are evidence for that rather than against it.
- DONE (V1/V5): **the importer served tests and nothing else - the product could not open a KiCad board.** `parse_kicad_pcb` had been fixed five times, and `cypcb check board.kicad_pcb` still handed the file to the DSL parser and answered `× Missing a definition` pointing at `(kicad_pcb`. The only command that touched the importer was `parse-kicad`, which prints metadata as JSON.
- `crates/cypcb-cli/src/board_source.rs` is the one place that decides which reader a file goes to. `check` and `export` dispatch on it, so a KiCad board now goes all the way through: **`cypcb export plane_board.kicad_pcb --preset jlcpcb` writes the full set and the imported ground plane reaches the bottom copper** - 51 `G36` regions in `plane_board-B_Cu.gbr`.
- The copper a file already carries is applied on import. `led_blink.kicad_pcb` has three segments and two vias; dropping them would read as an unrouted board and report every pin it connects as unreached.
- Zone refusals print as warnings on the way through, so a pour this importer will not approximate is said out loud by the command a person actually runs.
- Three tests run the real binary, and **all three fail with the dispatch removed**. Workspace 105 suites, gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **the copper already in a KiCad file arrived offset by the whole board origin.** Every pad has its position taken relative to the board's own corner - `parse_footprint` subtracts `board_origin` - and `parse_segment` and `parse_via` did not, because neither was given it. KiCad lays boards out on a sheet, so that corner is almost never at the file's origin.
- Found while looking at something else: `cypcb check led_blink.kicad_pcb` reported `edge-clearance: 5` on a board whose copper is nowhere near an edge. The traces were at 105-122mm on a 40 x 30mm board, because the file's outline starts at (95, 55) and its traces at (110, 62).

| `cypcb check led_blink.kicad_pcb` | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| edge-clearance | 5 | **0** |
| unrouted-pin | 12 | **8** |
| clearance | 0 | 3, one of them a 0.00mm short |

  The four pins that stopped being unreached are pins the file's own traces do connect. The three clearance violations are real facts about that hand-written fixture's copper, reported for the first time now that it is on the board.
- **It stayed invisible because the importer's copper only ever fed benchmarks, and the benchmark harness routes from scratch.** It became visible the moment `check` and `export` started reading KiCad boards one fire ago - a feature exposing a defect in the code it was built on.
- Two tests hold it: every imported trace and via is inside its board on all three fixtures that carry copper, and `led_blink`'s first segment lands at exactly (15, 7) to (20, 15), which is where (110, 62) to (115, 70) belongs once the corner is subtracted. Both fail with the subtraction removed.
- No ratchet moved, workspace 105 suites, gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **the loop closes - draw a board in KiCad, route it here, open it in KiCad.** `route` takes a `.kicad_pcb` and writes one back. The `.cypcb` path appends trace blocks to a copy of the source, and doing that to a `(kicad_pcb ...)` file would have produced something neither reader can open, so the KiCad input gets its own writer.
- **What is written is narrow on purpose:** the `(segment ...)` and `(via ...)` forms routing produces, inserted before the file's own closing paren. Footprints, setup, nets, zones this importer would not approximate - everything the project models loosely or not at all - is carried through byte for byte. The safest way to preserve what you do not model is not to rewrite it.
- Measured through the release binary on `plane_board.kicad_pcb`: `Wrote /tmp/rt.routed.kicad_pcb (210 segments, 25 vias) in 0.46s`, 235 forms added, coordinates back in file space - the board's corner is at (100, 60) and a segment at board (17.399, 22.733) writes as `(start 117.399 82.733)`.
- **The round trip is the proof, and it is exact.** Re-importing what was written: `unrouted-pin: 46` becomes none, and the DRC reads 39 clearance plus 18 shorts - the same numbers the ratchet measures for this board routed in memory. Copper written at the wrong origin would pass every structural check and connect nothing, so the test asserts the re-imported board has zero unreached pins; with the origin left out of the writer it fails with `46 before, 46 after`.
- A `ViaPlacement` carries a drill and no outer copper. Rather than invent a ring, the writer uses the project's existing answer - `cypcb-router` builds a `Via` with `drill * 2` under the comment "Default annular ring" - so the file and the model do not disagree about the same via.
- Workspace 107 suites, gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **the viewer opens a KiCad board.** The command line learned to read, check, route and write them; the browser could open the project's own `.cypcb` and nothing else, so somebody holding a `.kicad_pcb` had no way to look at it.
- The importer compiles to wasm32 - it needed no change, `parse_kicad_pcb_str` already existed beside the path-taking entry - and the cost is measured: **`viewer/pkg/cypcb_render_bg.wasm` 1,083,520 -> 1,125,978 bytes, +42KB on 1.08MB, 3.9%.**
- `PcbEngine::load_kicad` mirrors `load_source`: the engine reads, the snapshot carries everything the viewer draws, the copper already in the file is applied, and a pour the importer will not approximate comes back as a diagnostic rather than disappearing.
- It had to be wired through three layers, which is what took the time - the wasm export, the `WasmPcbEngine` interface, the `PcbEngine` interface and its adapter, plus the mock that stands in when the module fails to load. The first run failed with `engine.load_kicad is not a function` even though the binding was in the generated JS, because the viewer programs against its own interface and not against the raw engine.
- Two playwright cases: `plane_board.kicad_pcb` opens with **12 components, 51 pads and a 50 x 38mm board**, and the file input offers the extension - a reader that works behind a picker that will not offer the file is a feature nobody can reach.
- Gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **opening a KiCad board in the viewer put it in the editor, and the first character typed emptied the board.** The editor hands its content to `load_source` on every change, `load_source` clears the world before parsing, and a `(kicad_pcb ...)` file is not a `.cypcb`. That was a defect the previous fire introduced with the feature.
- The viewer records which language the open board is written in, and three places use it: the editor's debounce reloads with that reader, the file-load path sets it, and the recent-file merge no longer splices DSL trace blocks into a file that is not DSL - which would have stored something neither reader can open under the name of a board the user still has.
- **The first version of the guard passed with the fix reverted, and was thrown away.** It drove Monaco directly; the debounce did not run inside the harness, so it proved nothing. The replacement asserts what can actually be observed: opening a KiCad board records `kicad_pcb`, opening a `.cypcb` records `cypcb`, and the two readers really do disagree - the KiCad reader keeps all 12 parts and the DSL reader leaves 0. With the recording line deleted it fails with `Received: "cypcb"`.
- Gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **`plane_board` had two parts in the same place, and asking the question of every fixture found 28 more.** C1 and C2 sat 3mm apart while an 0603 courtyard is about 3mm wide, so they touched at exactly 0.00mm against 0.25mm required. Mine, four fires old, reported at the time and not fixed. Fixed at the generator - 4mm apart - so the file and the script that writes it agree.
- **Measured across all six before touching anything:** `multi_ic` 26 overlapping pairs, `stm32_breakout` 2, `plane_board` 1, the other three clean. Nothing had ever asked a fixture whether its parts fit beside each other.
- `no_fixture_asks_for_two_parts_in_the_same_place` now asks it, holding each board against a known count the way the off-board-pad check does. The 26 and the 2 predate this by a long way and fixing either moves its ratchet and every table drawn from it, so they are recorded rather than tolerated in silence.
- **I predicted this moves no ratchet and was wrong.** Moving one capacitor 1mm changed the routing: 210 routes became 181, violations 40 -> 28, shorts 18 -> 13. The ratchet is re-measured and tightened, 49/23 -> **37/18**. The band is carried over from the board before the move and the comment says so - a 1mm move does not change how sensitive a board is to the via price, but it was not re-measured, and the ratchet only tightens either way.
- Workspace 107 suites, gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **more than half those overlaps were the importer's invention, not the boards'.** Before moving a single part, the question was where the courtyard comes from: KiCad keeps it as `F.CrtYd` graphics inside a footprint, this reader carries pads only - `multi_ic` has no `F.CrtYd` line at all - so it has to be derived. It was derived as the pad bounds plus a flat **0.5mm**, twice the `IPC_COURTYARD_EXCESS` this project uses everywhere else, sitting under a comment about something unrelated.
- Every imported board therefore lost half a millimetre of apparent clearance between every neighbouring pair, and the checker invented overlaps the same board would not have if written in this project's own language.

| fixture | overlaps at 0.5mm | at the project's 0.25mm |
|---|---|---|
| multi_ic | 26 | **12** |
| stm32_breakout | 2 | **0** |
| plane_board | 1 | **0** |
| led_blink, shift_driver, qfp_fanout | 0 | 0 |

- `multi_ic` drops from 318 violations to **304** with the 14 phantom overlaps gone; every other ratchet is unchanged. Its ratchet tightens 381 -> 367, measured plus its band of 63.
- The 12 left on `multi_ic` are real placement in a hand-written fixture. They stay recorded in `KNOWN_OVERLAPS` rather than tolerated in silence, and repairing them still moves that fixture's ratchet, band and tables.
- Workspace 107 suites, gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **`multi_ic`'s twelve real overlaps are repaired, and every fixture is now a board whose parts fit beside each other.** Nine were decoupling capacitors ringing U1 at a uniform 0.14mm where 0.25mm is required - one ring placed slightly too tight, fixed by moving both columns 0.5mm outward. The other three were an HC49 crystal 13.4mm wide across its neighbours and two parts against the Ethernet transformer.
- **The crystal took three attempts because I was guessing at its size.** Moved to two "empty" spots it collided with three more parts each time; measuring the footprint - pads at plus and minus 5.7mm, so 13.4mm with its courtyard - placed it correctly first time after that. Read the part before moving it.
- Measured before and after on the unrouted board, so what the moves cost is visible rather than assumed:

| | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| courtyard-clearance | 12 | **0** |
| solder-mask-bridge | 28 | **20** |
| clearance | 39 | 38 |
| edge-clearance, unconnected-pin, unrouted-pin, shorts | 1, 2, 202, 32 | unchanged |

- Routed: 978 routes -> 945, violations 304 -> 291, shorts 177 -> **187**. A placement repair is not an optimisation - the shorts rose - and the board is buildable now where it was not. Ratchet 367 -> **354**; the shorts ratchet stays at 210 rather than the 220 measured-plus-band would give, because raising one to match a formula loosens it for no reason.
- `KNOWN_OVERLAPS` is empty. A new entry in it is now a new defect rather than an inheritance. The margin table in `docs/routing.md` is dated with the moved numbers.
- Workspace 107 suites, gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **the copper three fixtures shipped was not routing at all - it was straight lines between part centres.** `multi_ic`'s first segment runs from U4's origin through C1's to U1's, so it crosses every package on the way; the same decoration was on all three boards, shorting **1, 13 and 32 times** before anything was routed. Reading the coordinates was what settled it: `(65 45) -> (90 62) -> (100 70)` are part positions, not a path anybody laid.
- Removed: 3 segments and 2 vias from `led_blink`, 8 and 4 from `stm32_breakout`, 15 and 10 from `multi_ic`. All three now ship **zero** shorts.
- **`led_blink` keeps copper, and it is real copper**: one trace from R1 pad 2 at (120.48, 65) to D1 pad 1 at (124.25, 65), both on `LED_ANODE`, adjacent parts, nothing in between. The import-copper path still has a fixture to prove itself on, rather than losing the coverage with the decoration.
- **Every ratchet is unchanged**, which is the proof the decoration fed no measurement: the benchmark routes from scratch and never read it. What did read it was `cypcb check`, the viewer, and `board_source` - everything a person actually points at a board.
- Three test files leaned on it and were repointed rather than deleted: the parse expectations now say 1 segment and 0 vias for `led_blink` and no copper for the other two, and the origin test - which exists to catch copper imported without subtracting the board corner - measures against the new trace's real coordinates.
- Workspace 107 suites, gate green 8/8.
- **That claim was wrong when I wrote it.** Two fixtures still carried copper outside their own outline - `stm32_breakout` 8 pads, `shift_driver` 6 - recorded in `KNOWN_OFF_BOARD` and reported as an owner decision for a dozen fires. It was never an owner decision: every reason it was blocked on ("it moves ratchets and tables") is work I have since done four times without asking, on `qfp_fanout`, `multi_ic` and `plane_board`. The blocker was stale, and I kept repeating it.
- DONE: **both are repaired and `KNOWN_OFF_BOARD` is empty.** `stm32_breakout` had two ten-pin headers whose last four pins ran 7.9mm past its top edge; they moved from y = 90 to y = 80. `shift_driver` had three bypass capacitors 3.4mm above the board, placed there to clear a DIP courtyard; they moved to y = 1.5 inside it, fixed at the generator.
- **Making them fabricable improved the routing sharply, which is the argument for doing it:**

| board | routes | violations | shorts |
|---|---|---|---|
| stm32_breakout | 908 -> 899 | 239 -> **180** | 136 -> **93** |
| shift_driver | 700 -> 671 | 81 -> **65** | 33 -> 34 |

  Pads off the board had been forcing the router into the edge region. Ratchets tightened to measured-plus-band: 239 -> 210 and 93 -> 77. The shorts ratchets stay where they were rather than being tightened by a number nobody measured - neither board has a measured shorts band - so they are looser than they could be and the comments say so.
- Every fixture is now a board somebody could make, and this time the list backs it: no copper outside an outline, no parts in the same place, no invented copper, no plated mounting holes.
- Workspace 107 suites, gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **every ratchet and every band re-measured in one run, on boards that are all fabricable for the first time.** The shorts columns were being printed by the sweep all along and never read.

| board | routed | band | shorts band | ratchet |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| stm32_breakout | 180 / 93 | 59 | 61 | 239 / 154 |
| multi_ic | 291 / 187 | 65 | 56 | 356 / 243 |
| shift_driver | 65 / 34 | 17 | 8 | 82 / 42 |
| qfp_fanout | 309 / 147 | 57 | 44 | 366 / 191 |
| plane_board | 28 / 13 | **0** | **0** | 28 / 13 |

- **Three ratchets widen rather than tighten, and that is the correction.** The bands moved when the fixtures were repaired - `stm32_breakout` 38 -> 59, `qfp_fanout` 40 -> 57 - so the value I set last fire, 210 from a stale band of 30, sat *inside* that board's noise. A ratchet inside the noise is a test that fails for reasons unrelated to the change being made. `qfp_fanout` and `plane_board` tighten sharply for the same reason, 376 -> 366 and 49 -> 28.
- **`plane_board` routes identically at every price from 0.22 to 0.28** - 28 violations and 13 shorts, five times over - so its ratchet is the measured value exactly and any movement at all is real. A board with a ground plane has far fewer nets competing for the same cells, so the rip-up order does not change when the price does. Every board that wobbles is one where the negotiation has room to go differently.
- The band table in `docs/routing.md` is republished from this run, all five boards in one table with shorts, replacing a two-board table measured before any fixture was repaired.
- Workspace 107 suites, gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **`cypcb check` reported a pass on a file with no board.** Measured by checking all twenty examples as a set, which nothing had done: `examples/v2-interfaces.cypcb` came back `OK: passed DRC against jlcpcb_2layer in 0ms` while its board is literally `"board": null` with no components and no nets.
- **Every rule skips quietly when there is no board size** - `EdgeClearanceRule` says so in its own doc - so the checker ran, checked nothing, and called it a pass. That is fine for a file which is honestly a library of interface declarations. It is not fine for the case it cannot be told apart from: a design whose `board` block failed to parse, or whose import did not resolve, getting the same green line as a board that was actually checked.
- Now: a file with no board and no parts says `declares no board and places no components: nothing was checked` and exits 0, because a library is not an error. A file that **places parts and declares no board** is an error naming both - `places 1 component(s) and declares no board`. Three tests, and the first two fail with the guard removed.
- The sweep over all twenty examples also turned up things worth their own fires: `alignment-test` has copper 1 violation from the board edge, `custom-footprint` has 3 annular rings under minimum, `syntax.cypcb` - the syntax reference - has a clearance violation, and `v2-modules` has 2 courtyard overlaps. `drc-test` and `pour-island` are meant to be bad; those four are not.
- **`v2-constraints`' three failing assertions are the tool being honest**, not a defect: `assertion not checked: within needs tolerances, which the board model does not carry yet`. It says what it cannot do rather than passing.
- Workspace 108 suites, gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **five examples shipped faults somebody would copy, and each was the kind that teaches wrong.**

| example | fault | fix |
|---|---|---|
| `syntax.cypcb` | `VCC` left R1's **left** pad heading right, straight across R1's own right pad at 0.00mm | net and trace use R1.2 |
| `uat-routing-locked.cypcb` | the same thing, in the example that demonstrates locked traces | same |
| `custom-footprint.cypcb` | taught how to declare a footprint, using a 0.8mm drill in a 1mm pad: 0.1mm of copper where a fabricator makes 0.15mm | pads 1.6mm, a 0.4mm ring |
| `alignment-test.cypcb` | a 1206 at 2mm, so its left pad reached the board edge | moved to 4mm |
| `v2-modules.cypcb` | a module whose own capacitors sat 0.20mm from the regulator's courtyard against 0.25 required | moved to 3.5mm |

- **The fifth was found by the guard, not by me.** I read the sweep, listed four, and wrote the check; it failed on `uat-routing-locked.cypcb` immediately. A test written from a reading catches what the reading missed.
- `every_example_is_a_board_worth_copying` now checks all twenty as a set. `unrouted-pin`, `unconnected-pin` and `assertion` are excluded because they say what an unrouted fragment *is*; `drc-test` and `pour-island` are listed as deliberately bad, and the test fails if either becomes clean, so nothing joins that list quietly.
- Workspace 109 suites, gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **`score` printed a smoothness of 1.0 for every board ever routed, without examining a single corner.** `compute_smoothness` walked `trace.segments.windows(2)` and skipped any trace with fewer than two segments; `apply_routes` builds one entity per route segment - `segments: vec![TraceSegment::new(..)]`, in all four places it does so - so every trace had exactly one segment, every trace was skipped, and the function returned its `total_bends == 0` default.
- It now finds every point where copper on the same net and layer meets, and measures the turn there, with both directions taken **away from the joint** - using each segment's own start-to-end direction reads a straight run as a reversal whenever the router emitted the second segment pointing back at the first.
- **The value is still 1.0 on every board, and that is the honest result.** The router works on a 45-degree grid and `angle_penalty` charges nothing for a 45-degree turn. I expected the number to move and it did not; what changed is that 1.0 is now an answer rather than an accident. Three tests hold it: a 20-degree corner scores below 1.0, a 45-degree corner scores exactly 1.0, and straight copper through a joint scores 1.0. The first fails on the old code with `a 20-degree corner scored 1`.
- Every ratchet unmoved, workspace 110 suites, gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **`layer_balance` rewarded the opposite of its name.** It counted only the layers that carried copper, so everything on the top of a two-layer board gave one map entry, `min == max`, and a perfect 1.0 under the comment "single layer, balanced by definition".
- **Proven on a real ranking rather than argued.** The variant diagnostic did not print the metric, so it could not be judged; printing it showed `led_blink`'s `Low-Via` variant - zero vias, everything on one layer - scoring `balance 1.000` while every variant that spreads across both layers scores 0.200. The term subtracted 40 points from each balanced route and nothing from the single-layer one.
- Measured against the layers the board *has* now, with unused layers counting zero: `panel-mount` and `four-layer` go **1.0 -> 0.0**, `blink` stays at 0.286 because it genuinely uses both. Four tests hold the shape, including two layers of a four-layer stack scoring 0 where they used to score a perfect 1.0.
- Whether spreading is desirable at all is a different question, and the composite already answers it elsewhere by charging per via. This metric's job is to say what it is named for.
- Ratchets unmoved, workspace 111 suites, gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **`crossings` is correct, and the composite charges for it twice.** Unlike the other two it does vary - 0, 2, 1, 0, 4 across five example boards - so the question was whether it is right rather than whether it runs. It is: two traces crossing on one layer count 1, the same two on opposite layers count 0, which is what layers are for.
- **What nobody had noticed is that a crossing is also a short.** Measured on two traces crossing at a point: `crossings` 1, `drc_violations` 1, `shorts` 1. The composite pays 500 for the crossing and 1000 for the same contact called a short - 1500 for one place where two nets touch.
- I wrote "two clearance violations, one per trace" in the test's own comment before measuring; measuring said one. The comment is corrected, which is the whole reason to measure a thing you are sure about.
- Nothing has been retuned. Both numbers are right about what they measure, and the finding is that the terms are not independent, so a weight tuned on one moves the other. `docs/routing.md` now carries the whole weight table and what each term was found to be doing.
- Workspace 112 suites, gate green 8/8.
- **Three things measured and found sound**, which is worth recording so nobody re-measures them: `cypcb parse` reports a pour with its name, kind, net, layer mask and bounds; exporting a board with shorts is refused with exit 1 and writes nothing; `--force` writes the files with an explicit `Forcing:` line and exits 0. The last two already had a test in `cli_integration.rs`.
- DONE: **the output directory is what somebody zips and sends, and it silently accumulated other boards.** Exporting `four-layer` and then `blink` into one directory leaves twenty Gerbers for two different boards, including `In1_Cu` and `In2_Cu` copper belonging to neither the two-layer board being sent nor anything the fabricator was asked for. Nothing said a word.
- An export now names what it did not write: `Warning: 15 file(s) in /tmp/mix2 were not written by this export and will travel with it`, listing eight and counting the rest, ending with what to do about it. Re-exporting the same board stays silent, because overwriting its own files is the ordinary way to use the command.
- Three tests: a second board is reported and named, the same board twice is silent, a fresh directory is silent.
- Workspace 113 suites, gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **inner-layer copper reached the files and nothing checked that it did.** Measured first: a routed `four-layer.cypcb` exports `In1_Cu.gbr` with 2 draws, so the exporter is right. What was missing is the guard - `cli_integration` asserts the inner files are *named* in the export's output, which an empty file satisfies, and `routed_board_reaches_the_files` only ever covered top and bottom.
- `an_inner_layer_carries_its_own_copper_and_nobody_else_s` routes one trace per layer of a four-layer board at four different heights, so a layer carrying somebody else's copper shows in the coordinates rather than only in a count. **Proven to bite**: making the layer filter treat every inner layer as one - `trace.layer != layer && !matches!(layer, Layer::Inner(_))` - fails it with `In1 carries the trace that belongs to top`, while the old top-and-bottom test passes throughout.
- A four-layer board is where losing a layer is hardest to notice and most expensive: the fabricator builds the stack from what arrives.
- Workspace 113 suites, gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **a drilled pad did not exist on the layers it is drilled through, and two readers disagreed about which layer is which.** Pulling on the four-layer example's short found both.
- `sync.rs` gave every through-hole DSL pad exactly `[TopCopper, BottomCopper]` whatever the board declared, so on a four-layer board it was absent from In1 and In2: an inner trace could not reach it, and the checker could not see its copper there. `examples/four-layer.cypcb` - whose whole subject is inner copper - shipped a trace on Inner1 joining two pads it could not touch, and `cypcb check` had been saying so all along with **four pins unreached**.
- **The numbering split was the second half.** `job.rs` writes `Layer::Inner(n)` as `In{n + 1}` and the DSL reads `Inner1` as `Inner(0)`, so the project is zero-based inside - but the KiCad reader read `In1.Cu` as `Inner(1)` and the KiCad writer wrote `Inner(n)` as `In{n}`. An imported inner trace landed one layer deeper than the file said and would have exported under the next layer's name. Both are aligned on zero-based now, and `In0.Cu` is refused rather than read as `Inner(-1)`.
- Measured end to end on the example: **4 unreached pins -> 2**, the two left being the `CROSS` net which has no copper in the file by design. Its routed board went from `1 violations, 1 of them copper touching copper` to `0 violations` with a via, so it exports without `--force` now.
- The pads were also given a drill in the example, which is what the file always meant: a pad with no drill is surface copper on one layer and cannot be joined by an inner trace.
- Every ratchet unmoved, workspace 113 suites, gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **the chain a four-layer board takes is now checked from the file a person writes to the file a fabricator reads.** Both defects fixed last fire could hide because every test sat on one side or the other: the unit tests check `export_copper_layer` directly, and `cli_integration` checks that the inner files are named. Nothing ran a four-layer `.cypcb` through routing and export and looked at which Gerber the copper landed in.
- `a_four_layer_board_reaches_its_own_layers` does, with no `--force` - a board that needs it is a board that cannot be sent. Measured: `F_Cu` 1 draw, `In1_Cu` 2 draws, `In2_Cu` 0, `B_Cu` 0, and four pad flashes on every one of them.
- **Both defects are caught by it.** Reverting the drilled pad to the outer two layers gives `In1_Cu carries 1 flashes, and the board has four drilled pads that every copper layer passes through`; reading `Inner1` one layer deeper would put the trace in `In2_Cu`, which the third case forbids.
- The first assertion said "4 flashes exactly" and read a legitimate via ring as a defect. It asks for at least four now, which is what it means: a via is free to flash beside the pads.
- **The same shared-scratch-directory race caught me a third time** - three tests, one output directory, deleting each other's files, all of it looking like an export failure. Per-test directories, and the helper now says so in its own doc.
- Workspace 114 suites, gate green 8/8.
- DONE: **`In2_Cu` carries copper on a board in the repository for the first time.** `examples/four-layer.cypcb` gained a second pair of pads joined on Inner2, so both inner layers now hold a trace the file names: `F_Cu` 1 draw, `In1_Cu` 2, `In2_Cu` 2, `B_Cu` 0, with six pad flashes on every layer. The board still routes at **0 violations** and exports without `--force`.
- **The guard is by coordinate, not by count.** `SIG` sits at y=10mm on Inner1 and `SIG2` at y=5mm on Inner2, so a layer holding the other one's copper shows in the Gerber's own numbers. A draw count would have looked plausible either way - which is exactly how the off-by-one survived.
- Proven: shifting the DSL's `inner1` from `Layer::Inner(0)` to `Inner(1)` fails it with `In1 is missing the trace the file puts at y=10mm`, while the two structural cases beside it pass throughout.
- Workspace 114 suites, gate green 8/8.
- **The router does produce blind and buried vias, which nobody had measured.** On `multi_ic`: 119 vias, **46 of them blind or buried** across four layer pairs - 17 Top..In1, 14 In1..Top, 8 Top..In2, 6 In1..Bottom and one buried **In2..In1** that touches neither face. The claim that no board in the repository has one was wrong; no *example* does, and nothing checked the export.
- DONE: **the drill split is checked on a board the router actually produced.** The only coverage was `a_buried_via_is_not_drilled_from_the_outside`, which builds one via by hand. Routed and exported through the release binary, `multi_ic` writes five drill files - the through pass plus `Top-In1`, `Top-In2`, `In1-Bottom` and `In2-In1` - and the hits sum to **149 = 30 drilled pads + 119 vias**.
- **The first version of the second test asked the wrong question.** "Is any coordinate in two files" cannot tell a via written twice from two different vias the router stacked at one point: two coordinates appear in both the `Top-In1` and through passes, and `cypcb check` names one of them `USB_DP <-> OSC_IN` at 0.00mm - a genuine two-net short through one point, already reported as hole-to-hole. Counting is decisive where matching is not, so the test compares the total hits against the board's own vias and drilled pads.
- Fixed counts are deliberately not asserted: they move whenever routing moves, and a test that fails for that reason teaches people to update it without reading it.
- **The shared-scratch-directory race caught me a fourth time**, and again presented as a failure of the thing under test - the two cases passed alone and failed together. Per-test directories, with the reason in the helper's doc.
- Workspace 115 suites, gate green 8/8.
- **The stacked vias were chased and the obvious fix was measured and dropped.** After routing: `stm32_breakout` 4 hole-to-hole, `multi_ic` 7, `qfp_fanout` 15, most of them reading as a net against itself at 0.00mm.
- The plausible cause - a net's paths are converted to vias separately, so two paths changing layer at one point each ask for one - is **not** the cause. The drill files settle it: at (56.134, 41.910) one via goes into the `Top-In1` pass and the other into the through pass, so they have different layer spans and are repeats of nothing.
- Measured anyway, since the filter is four lines: deduplicating by position and span moved `qfp_fanout` from 309 violations to 291 and left **every hole-to-hole count identical**, 4, 7 and 15 before and after. It removes something, and that something is not what it was written for. Reverted - a change whose stated reason does not hold is one nobody can maintain, and 18 violations are not worth carrying a false explanation.
- Written up in `docs/routing.md` as the eighteenth instrument measured and dropped, with what a real fix would have to do: stop the search putting a via where the board already has one at any depth, which is a cost-model question rather than a filter on the output.
- DONE: **the cost model already has a knob for the stacked holes, and it is the wrong lever.** `CongestionMap` tracks the cells each via's ring covers and charges `ring_penalty` per ring; the default is **0.0**, so the search knows where every hole is and pays nothing to put another on top. That looked like the whole answer.
- Swept at 0, 1, 3 and 8 on the three fixtures that stack holes, with violations and hole-to-hole measured together in one run. **No price wins on both counts, and none wins on either consistently**: `stm32_breakout` improves at 8 (180 -> 158 violations, 4 -> 1 holes), `multi_ic` gets worse at stacking at every price tried (7 -> 13, 8, 12), and `qfp_fanout` halves its stacked holes at 1 while paying 67 violations, outside its band of 57.
- **The reason is what the penalty charges for.** A ring price makes the search avoid *crossing* copper around an existing via; it says nothing about *placing* a via on a cell that already has one. On a crowded board it pushes routes away from rings and creates the congestion that makes another layer change attractive somewhere else. Nineteenth instrument measured and dropped; the sweep stays as a diagnostic.
- Recorded with it: the 27 stacked holes this sweep reports for `qfp_fanout` and the 15 from `drc_report` are the same board through different harnesses - one checks against `DesignRules::jlcpcb_2layer`, the other against the `jlcpcb` preset - which do not share a hole-to-hole minimum. Rows compare within a table, never across.
- DONE: **`CELL_VIA` was never set because it cannot be**, and that is a hard constraint on the cost-model work rather than an oversight. `is_free` reads `self.layers[layer][idx] == CELL_FREE` - an equality against zero - so any bit set in a cell's byte is a hard veto. A flag written purely to record something blocks the cell that carries it.
- Measured rather than reasoned: recording each via on the layers it spans, with **nothing reading the flag**, moved every board. `qfp_fanout` 309 violations -> **383** and `plane_board` 28 -> 29, both past their ratchets; `multi_ic` 291 -> 222 and `shift_driver` 65 -> 49 the other way. A change that only writes a note should not have a before and after at all, and the ratchets caught the claim that it did not.
- Reverted; all six ratchets back at their committed values. Written up in `docs/routing.md` beside the two instruments already dropped chasing these holes.
- **Where the memory has to live instead:** beside `CongestionMap`'s ring array, which already carries per-cell counts the search reads as *cost* rather than as *permission*. That is the difference between the two structures, and it is the whole reason one of them can hold this and the other cannot.
- DONE and reverted: **the fourth attempt, built exactly where the third said it had to go.** `CongestionMap` gained a `holes` count beside `ring`, marked when a net's path is committed and unmarked on rip-up, read by the search as `via_stack_penalty` per hole at the one moment a via is *placed*. At 0.0 it reproduced every fixture byte for byte, which is the right way to add a knob.
- **At 5, 20 and 100 it also reproduced every fixture byte for byte** - identical violations, shorts, stacked holes, vias and routes. A term that costs a hundred and changes nothing is never charged, and I did not establish why before the budget ran out. The likely reading, recorded for whoever picks it up: a net's holes are marked only once all its paths are committed, so same-net stacking - `VCC_3V3` against `VCC_3V3`, most of these violations - cannot see itself.
- Reverted rather than shipped. A knob that provably does nothing is worse than no knob: it reads like a lever, moves nothing, and costs the next person a fire to discover that.
- Four attempts now, each narrowing the next, all in `docs/routing.md` with numbers: not the output filter, not the ring price, not the occupancy byte, and not this shape of hole count until the marking moment is understood.
- **One of the two candidates for the dead knob is eliminated, by reading rather than by rebuilding.** `variant.rs` copies seven `AutorouteConfig` fields and ends `..AutorouteConfig::default()`, which looked like the answer - anything a caller sets outside those seven is replaced. It is not: `via_price_sweep` sets `via_foreign_copper_penalty`, which is not among the seven, calls `route_board` directly, and gets a real spread of 33 to 42. The sweep that found nothing took the same route, so the plumbing carried the value.
- The seven-field copy is **not a defect either**, which is worth recording so nobody else chases it: what it drops falls back to the shipped defaults, and those are the measured values. The CLI has only two paths - `--fast` with plain defaults, or variants - and neither loses a tuned setting.
- So the remaining candidate is the marking moment: a net's rings, and the holes marked beside them, are recorded only once all of that net's paths are committed. That would explain same-net stacking not seeing itself; it does not explain the cross-net pairs, so it may not be the whole answer.
- **The cause is still not established, and this fire says so rather than guessing again.** My previous commit published the marking-time reading as "the most likely"; that was one candidate of two presented as if it were the finding, and the doc now says what is known and what is not.
- DONE: **instrumented instead of rebuilding, and the second candidate falls too.** Counting how often the via-transition branch prices a layer change at a cell already covered by a via ring: `multi_ic` **46,181 of 2,836,140** evaluations (1.63%), `qfp_fanout` **49,376 of 1,783,840** (2.77%).
- Tens of thousands of times per board the search evaluates putting a via where one already is, **with that information in front of it**. The congestion map holds it at the right moment, so the marking is not too late and the idea is not unworkable.
- **The fourth attempt therefore failed on its own wiring, not on principle.** A price at that decision point can work; what it needs first is a check that the hole array is actually populated - one assertion after routing one fixture, which the fourth attempt never made before trusting its sweep.
- It also explains the ring price: it charges all 46,181 of those evaluations for *crossing* copper, which is why it moved every board, and nothing extra for stacking, which is why it moved no stacked hole.
- Probe reverted; no code change. Four attempts, two eliminated causes, and the question is now narrow enough to be worth a fifth.
- DONE: **the fifth attempt found the bug, measured the price, and the price does not pay.**
- **The fourth attempt's bug was one line.** `congestion_map.set_stack_penalty(..)` was never added beside `set_ring_penalty` - my edit had matched no text and I never checked - so the map's penalty stayed 0.0 and `stacking_cost` returned zero however high the knob went. A price of 100 producing byte-identical boards was exactly that.
- Rebuilt with the check first, which is the lesson: routing `multi_ic` leaves **364 holes recorded for 119 vias**, about three layers each, so the map is populated *before* a price is swept against it.

| stack price | stm32_breakout | multi_ic | qfp_fanout |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 (shipped) | 180 / 4 | 291 / 7 | 309 / 27 |
| 5 | 208 / **0** | 245 / 15 | 368 / 31 |
| 20 | 184 / 2 | **237** / 9 | 366 / 31 |
| 100 | 203 / 1 | 237 / 9 | 371 / 30 |

- Violations then stacked holes, read against the bands 59, 65 and 57: `stm32_breakout` clears its stacking at 5 and gives back 28 violations; `multi_ic` is 54 violations better at 20 and *worse* at stacking; `qfp_fanout` is worse on both at every price. **No price wins.**
- **Kept at 0.0 rather than reverted**, unlike the fourth attempt, and the difference is that this one demonstrably works - every nonzero price moves the boards, the shipped default reproduces the router exactly, and a future sweep is one command. Same basis `via_ring_penalty` is kept on.
- NEXT-ACTION: none in this vector. Five attempts, and the honest summary is that stacked holes are not reachable by pricing the placement either - the two boards that improve and the one that worsens disagree in a way no single constant resolves, the same shape as the pad-zone margin. The next real move is a rule that varies with the board, and nothing here has established what it would key on.

### V3 - `cypcb-desktop`, the workspace member nothing compiles

- **Measured, and the exclusion is justified.** `scripts/quality-gate.sh` excludes `cypcb-desktop` from clippy and from the test run, and there is no CI in the repository at all - no `.github/workflows` - so the crate is compiled nowhere. Building it in the container fails on `pkg-config` and GTK, which is an environment limit rather than a defect, and the gate says so in its own comment.
- **What is covered, measured rather than assumed:** `cargo fmt --check` parses `src-tauri` and reports errors in it - a deliberate syntax error there fails stage 1 - so the sources cannot rot into unparseable code unnoticed. The logic the desktop wraps lives in `cypcb-platform`, which is in the tested workspace and passes 9 tests.
- **What is not:** type drift across those 251 lines. `src-tauri` imports exactly one project crate, `cypcb_platform`, so a change to that crate's `Menu`, `MenuBar` or `MenuItem` would break the desktop build and nothing in the repository would notice.
- Checked for this session specifically: nothing changed here touches `KicadPcbParseResult`, `PadDef`, `AutorouteConfig` or the layer naming, so none of the struct changes made over the last several fires can have broken it.
- NEXT-ACTION: none pulled. The desktop gap is now measured and bounded rather than unknown, and closing it properly means either a CI runner with GTK or a headless build of the crate - both are owner-scale decisions about infrastructure rather than fires.
- DONE: **components say which face they are on.** A `Side` component with the three questions a face answers - which copper its SMD pads take, which silkscreen it prints to, and its layer-mask bit. The KiCad importer reads `(layer "B.Cu")` from a footprint, which is the only place in the codebase where the side is data rather than inference; sync derives it from the footprint's copper and stores the answer so every rule reads the same one. The silkscreen rule takes it and falls back to the old guess only when nothing states it. Proven on a two-footprint board where one is `F.Cu` and one `B.Cu`: `[("R1", Top), ("R2", Bottom)]`.

### V2 - Autorouter and routing quality
- DONE: removed the rip-up retry loop in `astar_improved.rs` that could never run twice (every path returned or broke on the first pass - the author had already worked this out in a comment and silenced the lint instead of deleting the loop). Took the dead `max_iterations` parameter out of two functions with it. Behavior unchanged, `clippy::never_loop` unblocked.
- DONE: all 3 failing integration tests fixed by one defect. PathFinder's rip-up called `mark_route(cell, u32::MAX)` before `clear_route(net_id)` - that marks the cell occupied and erases its owner in the same step, so the clear found nothing and every ripped-up route left a permanent wall. `RoutingGrid::clear_cells` clears the recorded cells directly, which is also `cells.len()` work instead of a full `width * height * layers` scan per rip-up. blink.cypcb: `Partial { unrouted_count: 5 }`, 23 segments, 21.0mm, score 5036.0, 123.07s -> `Complete`, 45 segments, 6 vias, 182.5mm, score 212.5, 84.40s. Integration suite 432.26s -> 86.75s, 5 passed 0 failed.
