The type is kept now, alongside the string, and rides onto the board as a `TypedValue` component. `assert R1.value >= 47kohm` on a 10k part reports `but the board has 10000ohm`. A part written `value "10k"` is refused with the fix in the message: *write `value 10kohm` rather than `value "10k"`, or the checker has only a label to go on* - because "10k" is ten kilohms only if you already know the part is a resistor, and the checker should not be the one guessing.
- DONE: **assertions read nets.** `VBUS.current`, `VBUS.width` and `VBUS.clearance` resolve to what the net's own block states, which the board model has carried since M1. The field decides what kind of name it is - a part has a `value`, a net has those three - and `board` is neither, which is a collision the first attempt walked straight into: `board.width` matched the net branch and made a working assertion fail. A net that states nothing is not treated as satisfying everything; it is reported with the block to write.
- **Writing the test found a hole in the grammar.** `physical_unit` listed `uA` under a comment claiming `mA` and `A` were "already handled by current_unit". They were not listed, so `current 2A` parsed inside a net block while `assert VBUS.current >= 1A` was a syntax error. Both are units now. Proven end to end: `VBUS.current >= 1A` is silent on a 2A net, `VBUS.width >= 1mm` reports `but the board has 0.800mm`.
- DONE: **`import` works, so a module library is worth writing.** A path resolves relative to the file that wrote it - the way every other language reads a path in a source file, needing no project root to configure and no search path to get wrong. Only reusable definitions cross a file boundary: modules, footprints and interfaces. A board, a component or a net belongs to the design that wrote it, and letting those through would make `import` textual inclusion under another name. `import "x"` takes everything reusable, `import A, B from "x"` takes what it names and reports what is not there. Libraries can be built from libraries, and a file that imports itself is reported with the chain rather than followed. Wired into `check`, `score` and `route`, with import errors printed and the rest of the design still checked. Proven end to end: a `Divider` imported from `lib/blocks.cypcb` and instantiated twice passes DRC, and renaming it to one the library lacks reports `'lib/blocks.cypcb' does not define 'Regulator'`.
- DONE: **the language is visible now.** Everything shipped this week - modules, instantiation, placement, nesting, imports, assertions, typed values - existed only in tests. `examples/v2-modules.cypcb` defined two modules under a comment reading "Top-level board using the modules" and never used one; it instantiates three now, including a rotated one, and its internal nets are named so the pins actually map. `examples/lib/blocks.cypcb` plus `examples/v2-imports.cypcb` are new: a library with no board of its own, and a design that imports two blocks from it and places four instances. Both pass `cypcb check`, and all 17 examples still behave.
- DONE: **the README stopped saying the module system does not exist.** Two claims were false in the same document that describes the feature: "Where it's behind: no module/package system yet" and "The main gaps are copper pour, module system, and autorouter". The feature table now says what `module`, `use`, `import` and `assert` do and points at the examples; copper pour says what works (a zone carries a net, the ratsnest honours a pour) and what does not (nothing fills one); and the GitHub Actions row says CI is not planned rather than pending, which is the owner's decision from this session.
- DONE: **net classes.** `netclass Power [width 0.5mm clearance 0.3mm] { VCC GND }` states a rule once for a group of nets instead of repeating it on each. Precedence is the part worth getting right and is explicit: classes are applied before any net block, and a net that states something overwrites **only the field it states** - so `net VCC [width 0.8mm]` keeps the class's clearance. Everything downstream already honours per-net constraints, so the router draws a class's width and the checker enforces its clearance without another change. Proven end to end through assertions: on a board whose only statement is the class, `VCC.width >= 0.5mm` and `GND.current >= 1A` are silent while `VCC.width >= 1mm` reports `but the board has 0.500mm`.
- DONE: **the board has a real edge.** `BoardSize` is a width and a height, so a cutout, a slot and a chamfer all lived inside the same rectangle and edge clearance passed copper that sits outside the actual board. A `BoardOutline` ring answers that, the KiCad importer assembles one by walking Edge.Cuts segments end to end - in any order, either direction - and `EdgeClearanceRule` measures the box against the ring's edges, reporting zero for anything whose centre falls outside it. An outline that does not close yields nothing rather than a guess: a partial ring would put an edge where the board has none, and three sides of a rectangle is exactly what a half-finished file looks like. Proven on an L-shaped board where a pad in the removed corner is flagged and one well inside is not, and on Edge.Cuts written out of order with a segment reversed. **The benchmark fixtures now measure against an assembled ring rather than a bounding box and their numbers do not move** - 0/167/110 - which is the equivalence check for a rectangular board.
- DONE: **a board that is not a rectangle, end to end.** The outline had a model and an importer; it had no syntax and no way out to a fabricator, which meant an L-shaped board still exported as a rectangle - not a board with a cutout, but the wrong board. `outline { point 0mm, 0mm  point 40mm, 0mm ... }` writes one, and the Edge_Cuts gerber emits it. Proven on one file: a 40x40 board with the top-right quarter removed reports `edge-clearance at (30.000mm, 30.000mm): R2 ... 0.00mm actual` for the part standing in the notch, and its gerber walks six points and closes, where before it walked four corners of the bounding box. One ordering bug found by the board refusing to fail: the outline pass ran before the loop that creates the board entity, so `board_entity()` was empty and nothing was stored - it runs after now, which is the only order that can work.
- DONE: **a footprint can carry its own silkscreen.** The library held pads, bounds and a courtyard, so the only legend anything could reason about was the rectangle the exporter derived - which is why the silk-clearance rule measured a box rather than ink, and why a footprint fetched from a supplier arrived with artwork that had nowhere to go. `Footprint.silk` is a list of segments and circles in footprint coordinates. The checker measures those when they exist and falls back to the courtyard when they do not; the gerber prints them **instead of** the courtyard, because drawing both would put a box on the board the footprint never had. A circle is walked as a polygon fine enough that the error is under the width of the line drawing it. Proven with a part whose legend runs 4mm past its own courtyard onto a neighbour's pad: only the artwork path reports it.
- DONE: **a fetched footprint can bring its legend.** `register_footprint` took pads only, so the artwork a supplier sends had a home in the model and no road to it. It takes silk as well now, in the shape the viewer already uses - tagged by `type`, nanometres, relative to the footprint origin. An arc is parsed and dropped rather than refused: the board model has no arc, and rejecting a whole footprint over a rounded corner would be the worse trade. Malformed silk is an error rather than silence, like malformed pads. Two tests: a footprint carrying a segment, a circle and an arc keeps two shapes, and garbage comes back as `Failed to deserialize silk`.
- **Found while wiring it: the viewer cannot call `register_footprint` at all.** The interface method and the adapter passthrough went in with the parser swap and came out with it when that was reverted, so `registerDynamicFootprint` fills a JavaScript map and tells the engine nothing. `tsc` and vitest are clean because nothing references the missing method - which is exactly why it went unnoticed. Re-adding it belongs with the swap rather than before it, since the mock engine would need the method too and the mock is slated to go.
- DONE: **wired it, and the road runs both ways now.** `PcbEngine` declares `register_footprint`, the WASM adapter passes it through as JSON, and `registerDynamicFootprint` hands every fetch to the engine instead of only to the JavaScript map. A fetch that lands before the engine exists is replayed when it is created - a part fetched during startup used to be known to the drawing code and unknown to the model for the rest of the session. The test found a hole in the fix itself: the replay went into the WASM branch only, so the fallback engine still missed everything registered early. Both branches replay now. `npx vitest run` -> 14 files, 150 tests; `tsc --noEmit` and `eslint` clean.
- DONE: **and the copper arrives, which is the half wiring does not prove.** `crates/cypcb-render/tests/fetched_footprint_reaches_drc.rs` registers a two-pad part the way a supplier fetch does, places two of them 5mm apart and then 1.7mm apart, and the checker has to notice: pad copper reaches 0.8mm either side of a part's centre, so the close pair leaves a 0.1mm gap against the 0.127mm the preset asks for. Mutation-checked by registering the name and dropping the pads, which turns both tests red. The second one records something better than the failure it was written to catch: a board naming a footprint the engine does not know is **refused by name** rather than loaded padless and reported clean - pinned so nobody softens it into a warning.
- DONE: **a fetched arc is ink now instead of nothing.** A supplier's footprint draws rounded outlines and pin-one arcs, the board model has no arc, and `SilkInfo::Arc` was parsed and thrown away - so a part arrived with its artwork and printed without it, silently. Worse than that: the viewer sends `startAngle` and `endAngle` and the Rust struct did not have the fields, so the angles were dropped at deserialisation before the shape was dropped at conversion. `SilkInfo::Arc` carries both now (defaulted, so a payload written earlier still reads as the full circle it meant), and `to_shapes` walks it into segments at 32 to the turn - the same approximation the Gerber exporter already uses for a circle, where the polygon differs from the curve by less than the width of the line drawing it.
- **Found while testing it: the snapshot carries no silkscreen at all.** `build_snapshot` gives each component its pads, body size and 3D model and nothing about its legend, so the viewer draws silk from its own JavaScript copy of the footprint rather than from the engine. That is the same duplication V4 exists for, and it is why this had to be tested on the conversion rather than end to end.
- DONE: **it does now.** `ComponentInfo` carries the footprint's artwork, `SilkInfo::from_shape` describes what the model holds, and the field is omitted from the JSON when a part has no legend so a board without one costs nothing. The TypeScript side already declared `silk: SilkShape[]` on a component and filled it from its own parse - the engine simply never sent any. Proven on a registered part with a segment, a circle and a quarter arc: the snapshot comes back with **9 segments and 1 circle**, the arc having become eight of the segments. `SilkShape.layer` is optional now, because neither producer sets it and the renderer draws every shape in the silkscreen colour without asking - a required field nothing supplies is a type that describes nobody.
- DONE: **and closed the hazard that opened with it.** The viewer's JavaScript silk-clearance check read `shape.layer` to decide which face a legend prints on. The EasyEDA parser sets one; the engine does not, so the moment engine-supplied artwork reaches that check every shape would have compared `undefined` against every pad, matched nothing, and skipped silently - the exact shape of failure this project keeps finding. The component's own copper decides now when the shape says nothing. Mutation-checked: reading only the shape turns the new test red.
- **The larger dedup is still blocked.** Making the viewer draw from the engine's `silk` rather than its own is not a rendering change - `WasmPcbEngineAdapter.load_source` parses in JavaScript and builds the whole snapshot there, so the engine's components never reach the screen at all. That is V4 and it waits on the parser decision.
- DONE: **a footprint written by hand can carry its legend.** `silk line -1mm, 0.6mm to 1mm, 0.6mm width 0.15mm` and `silk circle -1.2mm, 0mm radius 0.15mm` inside a `footprint` block. A width left unstated takes the exporter's own stroke, so a legend written without one prints as the ink the exporter would have drawn anyway. Proven end to end: a part with one line and one circle emits **39** draw commands on the top silkscreen - two for the line, thirty-three for the circle walked as a polygon, four for the designator crosshair - and no courtyard rectangle, because a footprint with artwork prints that instead.
- DONE: **the command line can route with the project's own router, and the traces come back as source.** `cypcb route` shells out to a Java jar - its help says "using FreeRouting autorouter" and it wants `freerouting.jar` - while `score` uses the in-house PathFinder only to print numbers. So the README's headline promise, that traces persist as readable DSL code, had no command-line path at all. `cypcb route --in-house` routes with PathFinder, needs no Java, and writes the design plus its `trace` blocks to a new file. Proven on blink: 72 segments and 4 vias out, **14 `trace` blocks** in `blink.routed.cypcb`, and `cypcb check` parses that file straight back. The default stays on FreeRouting because which router this project bets on is D1's to answer, not mine.
- The writer moved rather than being copied: `export_traces_as_dsl` lived as a method on the WASM engine, which the CLI cannot reach. It is `cypcb_world::dsl::traces_as_dsl` now, the engine delegates to it, and its round-trip test moved with it - a value written and read back has to be the value that went in, which is what makes the DSL a storage format rather than a report.
- DONE: **every via was being lost on reload, and nothing said so.** Checking the second headline claim - that traces round-trip exactly - turned up a data-loss bug in the storage format. The writer emits each via as a block of its own, `trace GND { via 20mm,10mm drill 0.3mm }`, with no path in it. `sync_trace` decided whether to read geometry by testing `any(matches!(d, TraceDirective::Path(_)))`, so a block holding only a via never reached the branch that spawns one: route, save, reload, and every via was gone along with the layer change it carried. Silently. The predicate accepts a via now, and `crates/cypcb-world/tests/dsl_round_trip.rs` holds the line two ways - geometry in equals geometry out, and writing twice produces the same bytes, because a format whose output depends on how many times it has been through the tool is not a storage format.
- DONE: **one fixture that uses the whole language, and eight tests that it arrives.** The via bug was not a parser bug - the grammar read the via, the AST held it, and sync dropped it - and nothing caught that because no test asked whether what a file says is what the model ends up holding. `crates/cypcb-world/tests/language_conformance.rs` asks, for every construct there is: board and outline, an inline footprint with pads and silkscreen, components with placement, rotation, side and a typed value, net constraints and the class that fills in what a net leaves unsaid, a copper pour with its net beside a keepout without one, a pin-to-pin trace, a geometric one, a via block on its own, a placed and rotated module instance whose pin reaches the design's net, and the assertions. All eight passed first run. A construct added to the grammar without a line here is one nobody has checked reaches the board.
- **`examples/syntax.cypcb` is 28 lines and covers board, component, net and trace.** It is named as though it were the language reference and is a fifth of it. Left alone rather than grown, because the conformance test is the executable version and an example should read like a board someone would draw.
- DONE: **`setup-linux.sh` had a build host's own directory in it, and its last step could not do the one thing it was for.** Step 6 read `cd /workspace/codeyourpcb` - the path this project happens to sit in on the machine it is developed on. Under `set -e`, on anyone else's box, that line ends the script one step before the summary telling the reader what to run next. It changes to its own directory at the top now, and the whole script was run from `/tmp` to prove it: node, rust and the GTK check all found, stopping at `sudo: a password is required`, which is the container's limit rather than the script's.
- **The same step held a branch that could never run.** It called `cargo check -p cypcb-desktop` and then tested `if [ $? -eq 0 ]`, so with `set -e` a failing check exited the shell before the test - and `[WARN] Compilation check failed - may need additional dependencies` never printed, on exactly the machine that needed to read it. Demonstrated on its own: `set -e; false; if [ $? -eq 0 ]; then ...; fi; echo AFTER` prints nothing and exits 1. With the command in the condition, the real failing check now prints the warning and reaches the end: `[WARN] Compilation check failed`, `REACHED_END`, `exit=0`.
- `viewer/src/__tests__/the-setup-scripts-run-on-a-strangers-machine.test.ts` holds all nine setup/dev/build scripts to both rules. Mutation-checked on the previous commit: three of its sixteen tests fail. **Its first version failed on the fixed tree too** - it read the comment explaining the bug and matched the `$?` inside it, so it now strips comments before scanning: a test that cannot tell code from prose about code.
- DONE: **`DESKTOP-SETUP.md` named four installer files that will never exist.** It gives the reader exact paths - `CodeYourPCB_0.1.0_x64_en-US.msi`, `code-your-pcb_0.1.0_amd64.AppImage` and two more - while `src-tauri/tauri.conf.json` says `"version": "0.1.0-beta"`, which is the string Tauri builds the filename from. Every path was off by the suffix. They are replaced by the directories, plus the sentence that says where the name comes from: a document that repeats a version goes stale on the next release, and the build scripts already print what they produced.
- **Two size claims went with them.** "Instalator ~5-10MB", "Disk image ~8-12MB" and "mały (5-10MB)" are numbers nobody has measured - no bundle has ever been built from this tree - so they are gone rather than carried. What is checkable was checked and is true: `F11` for fullscreen and `Ctrl+Shift+T` for the theme are the shortcuts `menu.rs` binds, all nine setup/dev/build scripts the page names exist, and the menu bar really does have File, Edit, View and Help.
- **The first version of that guard passed against the very document it was written for.** `\b\d+\.\d+\.\d+\b` finds nothing in `CodeYourPCB_0.1.0_x64` because `_` is a word character, so the test reported green on the broken doc. Without the boundaries it fails on the previous commit with `the page names 0.1.0, tauri.conf.json says 0.1.0-beta`. A guard has to be run against the fault before it is trusted.
- DONE: **the desktop build had never worked from the scripts that build it, and they reported success anyway.** Running `./build-linux.sh` in a fresh checkout: `Couldn't recognize the current folder as a Tauri project. It must contain a tauri.conf.json ... in any subfolder`, then `Build complete!`, then `AppImage (portable): (not created)`, then `SCRIPT_EXIT=0`. Two faults in one run, neither reachable by compiling anything.
- **The first is a path.** The Tauri CLI looks for `tauri.conf.json` in the current folder or below it, and `src-tauri` is a **sibling** of `viewer`, not a child - so `npm run build:desktop`, which runs with `viewer` as its directory, could never find the project on any machine, with or without the system libraries. `dev:desktop` and `build:desktop` change to the repo root first now. Proven: from the root the CLI gets as far as `Running beforeBuildCommand`, and `--config ../src-tauri/tauri.conf.json` from `viewer` does **not** help - project detection reads the directory, not the flag.
- **The second is a lie.** `build-linux.sh`, `build-macos.sh` and `build-windows.bat` each ran the build, ignored its exit code and printed `Build complete!`. They check it now and say `Build FAILED` with what to try. Re-run after both fixes, the same command fails on the real reason - `The pkg-config command could not be found` - and exits **1**, with the advice pointing at `setup-linux.sh`, which does install `pkg-config` and the GTK set the README names.
- `viewer/src/__tests__/the-desktop-scripts-do-what-they-print.test.ts` holds both: the npm scripts have to leave `viewer` before running Tauri, and every build script has to name a failure before it can claim a success. Mutation-checked on the previous commit: **six of its seven tests fail**.
- DONE: **the desktop app shipped an Edit menu where five items did nothing.** `src-tauri` is excluded from every test run - it needs system GTK and webkit, and `cargo check -p cypcb-desktop` in this container stops at `The pkg-config command could not be found`, so the exclusion is honest and the crate is read by nothing. Reading it as text found the gap: the native menu declares an id per item, `menu.rs` acts on `file.quit` and `view.fullscreen` itself and emits the rest as `menu-action`, and `desktop.ts` switches on nine of them. **`edit.undo`, `edit.redo`, `edit.cut`, `edit.copy` and `edit.paste` fell through to `default:` and wrote a console line.** Undo and redo are real features whose keyboard shortcut has always worked, so the menu entry did nothing while `Ctrl+Z` did - the kind of gap only somebody running the desktop build would find.
- Undo and redo are wired to the app's own undo stack. Cut, copy and paste are **removed from the menu** rather than routed somewhere: they belong to whatever has focus, the code editor and every input already bind them, and a clipboard command aimed at nothing is worse than no menu entry.
- **Wiring them turned up a third copy of the same body and a drift between the first two.** Undo was written out at the toolbar button and again in the keyboard handler, and the toolbar copy forgot `interactionState.selectedTraceId` - so undoing from the button left a trace selected for dragging in the interaction layer after the entity it named was gone. One `performUndo` and one `performRedo` now, called from the toolbar, the keyboard and the desktop menu.
- `viewer/src/__tests__/every-native-menu-item-does-something.test.ts` compares the ids `menu.rs` declares against the handlers on both sides, in both directions, without a compiler - which is the only kind of check this crate can have here. Mutation-checked on the previous commit: `menu entries that emit an event nobody handles: edit.undo, edit.redo, edit.cut, edit.copy, edit.paste`.
- NEXT-ACTION: `interface` is the last v2 construct that parses and does nothing.
- QUEUED: DSL v2's other constructs still parse and do nothing - `interface` and `import`.
- QUEUED: DSL v2's other constructs still parse and do nothing - import resolution, interfaces, assertions.
- QUEUED: copper pour / ground planes - **the claim that no `Zone` type exists was wrong**, see M2 in the domain-model register; the gap is that a zone cannot name its net. KiCad `.kicad_pcb` export (import exists), parts engine, schematic generation, differential pairs, polygon board outline editing.

### V6 - Documentation truth
- DONE: **a working shortcut that no user could find.** `CLAUDE.md` calls itself the canonical shortcut registry and asks that every new one be added to it and to the help modal. `Ctrl+L` - simplify the selected trace - reached neither, so it has worked for as long as its handler has existed and only its author could use it. Listed in both now, and a vitest case reads the keys the handlers actually compare against and requires the registry to know each: it reports `keys the code handles and CLAUDE.md does not list: l` when the entry is taken away. A registry that is only as good as the last person's memory is not a registry.
- DONE: **the syntax guide taught a component property the parser has never accepted.** `docs/SYNTAX.md` documented `pin.<N> = <NET>` as an optional way to name a component's nets, with a worked example - `pin.1 = VCC` - and the grammar has no such rule: connections are declared the other way round, in a `net` block. A reader who typed the example got a syntax error and no way to tell whether they had mistyped it or the guide was wrong. Removed, with a line saying it never worked so nobody restores it from memory. The new test parses every concrete example in the guide as a document - 10 of 34 blocks; the rest are templates written with `<placeholders>`, bodies elided as `{ ... }`, or blocks the guide deliberately shows as invalid, and each skipped block is named in the output rather than quietly passed over. It fails on the previous commit.
- DONE: **`docs/architecture.md` was missing the largest crate in the workspace.** Thirteen crate entries each carried a `**Size**: ~N lines` snapshot and every one had drifted - `cypcb-drc` said 1,800 against a measured 7,221 - so they are replaced by the command that answers the question. Worse than stale numbers: **`cypcb-autoroute` (9,700 lines) and `cypcb-rules` (3,133) had no entry at all**, which is the router this project has spent most of its measurement on and the preset table every command now resolves `--preset` through. Both are written up, with dependency lists checked against their `Cargo.toml`. A diff of the doc's sections against `crates/*/` is now empty.
- DONE: **read `README.md` against the code, and four of its claims were out of date.** The feature table called silkscreen clearance a stub that runs in the viewer's JavaScript; it is a Rust rule and has been since the stubs were cleared. The benchmark table said 0 / 137 / 64 against a measured 2 / 271 / 210, from before the checker stopped merging faults, started measuring copper rather than part bodies, and began seeing footprints on imported boards - so the table now shows violations before routing, after, and how many the router introduced, with a line saying why the numbers went up while the boards did not get worse. The CLI section showed two commands of six and no flags; it shows all six, and that `check`, `route`, `score` and `export` share `--preset` and agree on the same board. The status paragraph named copper pour and a hidden button as the gaps and now names silkscreen text too, and says what the end-to-end chain actually checks. Every command block in it was run as written: `route --variants` then `score` on the file it produced reports 3 both ways.
- DONE: nothing this cycle.
- DONE: the README feature table now matches what the code does. Corrections: DRC lists the nine checks that actually run and marks silkscreen clearance as a stub living in the viewer's JavaScript; the desktop app no longer claims a working Linux build; the autorouter is listed with its toolbar button hidden; KiCad `.kicad_pcb` import moved from Planned to Done (`cypcb parse-kicad` returns JSON for the benchmark fixtures) with export left as Planned; the module system is "parsed, not instantiated" rather than absent. Added the measured routing quality table so a reader sees 0/137/64 instead of guessing.
- DONE: **`.gsd/REQUIREMENTS.md` and `STATE.md` agree with the tests again.** The counter said "23 active, 0 validated" while the file listed 14 validated - and running the tests showed the file was wrong in the other direction too. Five requirements were marked validated on the strength of E2E suites that no longer run: R110, R111, R112 and R113 rest on a tuning and variant panel whose 14 tests **skip** because the Route toolbar group is hidden, and R115's 3 screenshot tests **fail** for the same reason. All five are `active` now, each saying which half holds and which does not. R107 went the other way - it was `active` with a note that led_blink's 5 remaining violations were "grid artifacts", and it is validated at **0** with a gate that asserts equality. R101's note that no `Zone` type exists is gone. Counter now 13 active, 10 validated, 3 deferred, 2 out of scope, with the command to re-count beside it.
- **The counter that started this cannot be fixed by a commit.** `.gsd/STATE.md` is gitignored (`.gitignore:72`) and was deleted from the index in `3ac3947`, so it is a local working file: `git ls-files .gsd/STATE.md` returns nothing. Its "23 active, 0 validated" line was never in the repository and never will be, which is why it drifted with nobody noticing. It has been corrected on disk and given the command to re-count itself, and the versioned truth is `.gsd/REQUIREMENTS.md`, where each status now names the evidence.
- DONE: **read `.gsd/PROJECT.md` and `KNOWLEDGE.md` against the code, and both were overstating it.** PROJECT.md advertised "Comprehensive DRC (12 rules: ...)" and named twelve; the registry holds fifteen - `grep -c "Box::new(rules::" crates/cypcb-drc/src/lib.rs` -> 15. The line is a command now, per the docs convention that a count which moves without a schema change ships as the query rather than the answer. K010 states that clearance must resolve to per-pad entities with their own `NetId`; half of that is now true and the half that is not is written into the entry rather than left to be discovered - the geometry resolves to pads, the same-net exemption is still per component, so a trace on GND is exempt from every pad of a part with one GND pin, including its VCC pad. K011 requires rotated pad bounds instead of `max(half_w, half_h)`; the checker obeys it and `RoutingGrid::populate_pads` still does not, which is recorded at the rule.
- DONE: **read `.gsd/DECISIONS.md` against the code, and four of the twelve DRC decisions are not what the code does.** D-DRC-001 (per-pad ECS entities) was never built - the behaviour arrived another way, through pad geometry inside `ClearanceRule`. D-DRC-002 (rotated pad AABB) is half done: the checker obeys it, `RoutingGrid::populate_pads` does not. D-DRC-005 (12 rules, 2 stubs) and D-DRC-006 (silk checked in JS) are superseded. And **D-DRC-012 is the one that cost something**: it specifies courtyard entries at `layer_mask = 0`, no index builder in the crate does that, and `CourtyardClearanceRule` filtered for exactly that value - matching nothing, reporting zero, on every board it ever ran against. Each is annotated in place with what is true instead. A decision recorded and not implemented is worse than one never made, because the code downstream trusts it.
- DONE: **`REQUIREMENTS.md` read against the code, and R107's validation line was the stalest thing in the repo.** It claimed "DRC reduced from baseline 50 to 5 on led_blink... remaining 5 are grid artifacts" and that the gate asserts `<= 5`. Every part of that is out of date: the router introduces 1 on led_blink, 174 on stm32_breakout and 79 on multi_ic; the gate asserts `<= 1`; and the "grid artifacts" reading is measurably false - led_blink's one violation is a GND trace lying across C1's `SW_OUT` pad, a short on a board whose whole job is to blink. R204's validation said "unmapped" when `benchmark_validation` has asserted `unrouted == 0` for weeks, and its suspected root cause was recorded without the answer that was found: a net's own junctions counted as overuse because each connection marked the shared cells again.
- DONE: **the front door sent every new user to a port nothing listens on.** `vite.config.ts` has served **4321** for as long as it has existed; `README.md`, `CONTRIBUTING.md` and `viewer/start.sh` all said **5173**. Run from a fresh clone, `start.sh` prints `Open http://localhost:5173 in your browser` and Vite's own banner three lines later says `➜  Local:   http://localhost:4321/` - the program contradicting itself on screen. `.gsd/milestones/M001/slices/S07/tasks/T03-SUMMARY.md` had recorded "Dev server runs on port 4321 (not 5173 as documented in plan)" and nothing user-facing was ever corrected. All three fixed; `src-tauri/tauri.conf.json` was already right, which is why the desktop shell worked while the web quickstart did not.
- **Every command in the README's CLI block was executed from a fresh clone, in the order it is written**, and two were wrong. `parse examples/blink.cypcb` is commented `# the AST` and prints the board model - that changed when `-o json` was made to print what it always promised, and the README kept the old sentence; the line now shows both forms. `parse-kicad board.kicad_pcb` names a file that does not exist, so a reader who copies it gets `I/O error: No such file or directory`; it points at `tests/fixtures/benchmark/led_blink.kicad_pcb`, which returns `"component_count": 7`. The other five run as documented, including `route --variants` writing the file the `score` line reads.
- **The fresh clone itself is sound**, which had not been checked since the tree-sitter removal: `git clone` into an empty directory, `cargo build --release -p cypcb-cli` finishes in 30.65s with no C toolchain, `npm install` succeeds, and `./start.sh` builds the wasm and serves the app.
- `viewer/src/__tests__/the-front-door-names-the-right-port.test.ts` pins every claim to `vite.config.ts` rather than to the other documents, and checks Tauri's `devUrl` with them. Mutation-checked on the previous commit: three of its four tests fail with `expected 5173 to be 4321`.
- NEXT-ACTION: none. The four GSD documents have each been read against the code once, and the README has now been run rather than read.
- QUEUED: `.gsd/REQUIREMENTS.md` never received the M005 status writeback - `STATE.md` counts "23 active, 0 validated" while REQUIREMENTS lists 14 as validated. Empty DRC checkers (`trace_width.rs:53`, `solder_mask_bridge.rs:27`, `silk_clearance.rs:26`, `hole_to_hole.rs:37`) count toward "12 checkers" while doing nothing.

### V7 - Performance (GP-002 discipline: measure, then optimize, publish before/after)
- DONE: first baseline, and it found a 28x. `benchmark_routing_time` reported blink.cypcb at **93,459ms**. A per-iteration probe showed PathFinder shrinking the overused set for three iterations, then re-routing the same four nets for the remaining 46 - never converging, always burning the full budget, with each iteration slower than the last as the history cost climbed. Stopping after three iterations without a new best: **93,459ms -> 3,366ms**, routing-test **8,459ms -> 274ms**, both with byte-identical output. Regression gate 21s -> 1.12s; whole autoroute suite 44s.
- DONE: found why those four nets never separated - they were `DIS`, `TRG_THR`, `VCC`, `GND`, all multi-pin. `net_path_cells` gathers every connection of a net into one list and `mark_net` increments per entry, so a net's own junctions pushed occupancy to 2 against a capacity of 1 and stayed overused forever. The router was negotiating against itself. Deduplicating a net's cells before marking: blink **3,366ms -> 1,288ms**, routing-test **274ms -> 72ms**, output identical. Cumulative against the 2026-08-05 baseline: **93,459ms -> 1,288ms, 72x**.
- DONE: measured both. blink.cypcb now **converges in 3 iterations** (overuse 19 -> 1 -> 0) where before it never converged and always burned all 50; the stagnation break is a safety net now, not the normal exit. led_blink.kicad_pcb converges on the **first** iteration - zero cells shared between nets - and still reports 3 clearance violations, which settles what they are: not grid contention but the physical overlap of neighbouring cells, since a trace is 0.127mm wide and cells sit 0.0635mm apart.
- **The 3 violations on led_blink were never the story.** Running the DRC diagnostic across every fixture:

| fixture | routes | violations | clearance | edge | hole-to-hole |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| led_blink | 22 | 3 | 3 | 0 | 0 |
| stm32_breakout | 668 | **312** | 273 | 22 | 17 |
| multi_ic | 833 | **383** | 358 | 15 | 10 |

  The CI gate only ever looked at led_blink, so a router that produces hundreds of 0.00mm shorts on a realistic board looked healthy. `benchmark_all_fixtures_drc` now routes all three and ratchets each one; it is `#[ignore]`d because the two big fixtures take about two minutes each, and `scripts/quality-gate.sh` runs it explicitly in the benchmark stage.
- **Measured: a finer grid is not the answer.** Routing led_blink at four resolutions, with everything else held:

| resolution | routes | unrouted | DRC | length | time |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 63.5µm (default, clearance/2) | 22 | 0 | 3 | 79.61mm | 0.42s |
| 42.3µm (clearance/3) | 50 | 0 | 2 | 79.60mm | 0.93s |
| 31.8µm (clearance/4) | 47 | 0 | 3 | 79.57mm | 1.65s |
| 21.2µm (clearance/6) | 11 | **2** | 0 | 32.50mm | 1.94s |

  Violations do not fall as cells shrink, and the finest grid only reaches zero by abandoning connections - exactly what the completeness gate exists to catch. So the geometry of the grid is not the problem: **a route marks one cell per path node regardless of how wide the trace actually is**. At 63.5µm cells a 0.127mm trace covers two of them, one gets marked, and the neighbouring net legally takes the other.
- DONE: obstacles are bloated by clearance **plus half a trace** now. The grid tracks a route's centre line, so a node sitting exactly `min_clearance` from a pad still has half its copper inside that gap - which is precisely the 0.07mm-against-0.127mm violation on led_blink. One term in one expression:

| fixture | violations before | after | routes before | after |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| led_blink | 3 | **2** | 22 | 27 |
| stm32_breakout | 312 | **299** | 668 | 701 |
| multi_ic | 383 | **297** | 833 | 902 |

  All three still complete, and with *more* copper laid, so this is not the router winning by giving up. multi_ic composite 426,138 -> 338,113. Ratchets tightened to the new numbers.
- **Third failed experiment:** reserving the full trace footprint at marking time (every cell within `trace_width/2 + clearance/2` of a path node, via the occupancy mechanism rather than a veto). led_blink went 3 -> 4 violations, 22 -> 20 routes. Same outcome as both veto attempts. The reservation is right in principle but it fights the A* cost model, which still plans on centre lines; whoever picks this up needs to change both together, or nothing.
- DONE: classified the remaining violations and followed the biggest group. On stm32_breakout: 114 trace-trace, 114 trace-via, 19 pad-trace, 35 via-via - **over half involve a via**, and 243 of 285 sit at exactly 0.00mm. The grid marked a via as a single cell while its ring is 0.554mm across, about nine cells, so other nets routed straight through it. Feeding the via's real footprint to the **congestion map** - as cost, through occupancy - rather than blocking those cells:

| fixture | before | after | unrouted |
|---|---|---|---|
| led_blink | 2 | **1** | 0 |
| stm32_breakout | 299 | **238** | 0 |
| multi_ic | 297 | **240** | 0 |

  **This is the answer to three failed experiments.** Blocking cells (the two vetoes and the trace-footprint reservation) always traded completeness for correctness; the hard-blocked version of this very change left stm32_breakout with 6 unrouted connections. The same geometry expressed as a price the router can negotiate keeps every board complete and still removes a fifth of the violations. Ratchets tightened to 1/238/240.
- **Fifth failed experiment:** pricing every trace's footprint in the congestion map, the way the via footprint works. led_blink 1 -> 2 violations, stm32_breakout 238 -> 342, multi_ic 240 -> 263. Congestion pricing needs the priced feature to be *sparse*: a few dozen vias give a sharp signal, while every path node claiming a disc floods the map and the signal stops discriminating. Reverted.
- DONE: **the routing grid is a track pitch now, not half a clearance.** Every experiment so far tried to patch around a grid whose neighbouring cells are closer than two traces may be. Measured across resolutions on stm32_breakout: 238 violations in 127.8s at clearance/2, 193 at clearance, 199 at 1.5x, and **124 in 9.7s at `min_trace_width + min_clearance`**. One cell per legal track position means adjacency is clearance-legal by construction.

| fixture | violations before | after | time |
|---|---|---|---|
| led_blink | 1 | 1 | - |
| stm32_breakout | 238 | **124** | 127.8s -> 9.7s |
| multi_ic | 240 | **116** | - |

  All three complete, lengths within 1%, vias down (43 -> 23 on stm32). The whole all-fixtures gate now runs in 21s against 339s. Ratchets tightened to 1/124/116.
- DONE: **led_blink routes with zero DRC violations.** Re-classifying showed trace-trace had become the biggest group (64 of 106 on stm32, 56 of 100 on multi_ic), still ~80% at exactly 0.00mm - which on a track-pitch grid can only be diagonal corner-cutting: two diagonals crossing between cells without ever sharing one. Refusing to cut a corner past another net's copper: led_blink 1 -> **0**, multi_ic 116 -> **64**, stm32_breakout 124 -> 137.
- **stm32_breakout regressed by 13 and the ratchet was raised deliberately.** Routes that used to slip past each other diagonally become layer changes instead - vias went 23 -> 53 on that board. Measured alternative: `via_cost` 4.0 gives stm32 95 and multi_ic 91, but costs led_blink its zero (0 -> 3). No single default wins on all three, and tuning a product default to three fixtures is not the answer, so the default stays at 1.0 and the trade-off is recorded here:

| via_cost | led_blink | stm32_breakout | multi_ic |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1.0 (shipped) | **0** | 137 | 64 |
| 4.0 | 3 | 95 | 91 |

- DONE: re-classified. stm32_breakout is now dominated by **trace-via, 66 of 119 pairs**; multi_ic by trace-trace (23) and pad-trace (21). Owning the via ring outright instead of only pricing it - a two-cell disc on the coarse grid, against eight on the old one - was measured: stm32_breakout **137 -> 108**, multi_ic **64 -> 78**, led_blink unchanged at 0, all complete. Reverted: it trades one board against the other, and the ratchet discipline exists so that trade is not rationalised into the codebase.
- **Six experiments in, the pattern is the finding.** Every geometry lever - vetoing cells near foreign copper (twice), reserving trace footprints, pricing trace footprints, owning via rings - improves one benchmark board and worsens the other by a similar amount. The grid-level fixes that *did* land (track pitch, corner-cut refusal, obstacle bloat, via pricing) all improved every board at once. That difference says the remaining ~200 violations are no longer a geometry problem but a **cost-model** one: which conflicts the router should accept where.
- **Seventh failed experiment, and the one that was meant to work: the post-route repair pass.** Route, run the real DRC, forbid exactly the cells it complained about, route again, keep the result only if the violation count drops and the board stays complete. Built it (`route_with_blockers` on the strategy, a `Blocker` carrying a point and a layer mask, an accumulate-and-retry loop). Then measured the same binary on the same input three times:

| run | stm32_breakout, 2 repair passes |
|---|---|
| 1 | 115 |
| 2 | 135 |
| 3 | 129 |

  Against a baseline of 137. multi_ic never improved in any run (64 every time) and led_blink was already at zero, so repair exits immediately there. Reverted. A pass that cannot reproduce its own number is not shippable, and shipping it off-by-default is how this project ended up with empty DRC checkers and a disabled A*.
- **The measurement left a sharper question than the feature did: the router is deterministic without extra obstacles and nondeterministic with them.** Three runs, fresh process each, plain routing: byte-identical, 858 routes / 53 vias / 1630.67mm / 137 violations. Add blocked cells and the same three runs diverge. `extract_ratsnest` sorts by net ID, `order_nets` is a stable sort over it, `nets_needing_reroute` and the congestion map iterate Vecs - the entropy is somewhere else and is worth finding, because a router that answers differently each run cannot be ratcheted at all.
- DONE: **found it, and it was never the router.** The router is reproducible: three processes × three sequential routings on a world already holding applied traces gave byte-identical signatures on led_blink and stm32_breakout. What was not reproducible was **the DRC report itself** - two processes, same board, 308 violations both times and **152 of the printed lines different**: pair orientation flipped, and the reported coordinate moved. Chain: `apply_routes` grouped segments in a `HashMap<(NetId, Layer), _>` and spawned from it, so entity IDs came out in hash order; the spatial index is built over those entities; the clearance rule takes `location` from whichever of the two features the outer loop happened to be holding. Fixed at all three levels - spawn sorted by (net, layer), the violation reported lowest-entity-first with its location at the midpoint between the two features, and `run_drc` sorting its output canonically. Verified: two fresh processes, **0 differing lines**, counts unchanged at 0/176/127. `Layer` gained `Ord` for the sort.
- DONE: **the violation coordinate pointed at nothing.** `trace_to_trace_distance` found the minimum over every segment pair, then the rule threw that away and reported the centre of whichever spatial entry the loop was holding. Measured on the same violation, `trace 'PA5' ↔ trace 'PA6'`: reported at **(18.860, 46.291)mm**, actually at **(4.953, 64.897)mm** - 23mm away on a 100mm board. Click-to-zoom has been landing on unrelated copper. `segment_closest` now returns the midpoint of the two closest points along with the distance, and the location travels with the measurement through both trace paths. Counts unchanged (0/176/127), report still byte-identical across two processes.
- **Repair, measured a second time on a report that finally means something.** With the contact point fixed the pass does work - and it still does not earn a default:

| block radius | stm32_breakout (176) | multi_ic (127) |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | **167** | 127 |
| 1 | 176 | 127 |
| 2 | 176 | **110** |
| 3, 4, 6 | 176 | 127 |

  Every board stays complete, and the accept-only-if-better gate means a repair pass can never ship a worse board - only a slower one, about 3x the routing time. But radius 0 helps one board and radius 2 the other, which is the same shape as the six geometry experiments before it. Reverted again; the code was written twice now and is in the history at `450d734^..` if a third attempt wants it.
- DONE: **the repair pass ships.** Written and reverted twice for the same reason - one radius helped one board - the third version stops choosing. Each radius in `repair_block_radii` is an independent attempt from the same starting board and the measured winner is kept, so there is no constant to tune to a fixture. Defaults: 2 passes over radii `[0, 2]`.

| fixture | before | after | routing time |
|---|---|---|---|
| led_blink | 0 | 0 | 0.07s -> 0.07s (exits at zero, pays nothing) |
| stm32_breakout | 176 | **167** | 6.1s -> 27.2s |
| multi_ic | 127 | **110** | 23.5s -> 69.1s |

  Every board complete, none worse - the pass keeps a candidate only when the count drops and the board still routes, so the worst it can cost is time. One pass buys nothing on either board; the win lands on the second, once a rejected attempt's cells have joined the next one. `benchmark_all_fixtures_drc` now routes through `route_board` rather than the bare strategy, because repair is what a user gets when they press Route and the ratchet has to measure what ships. Ratchets 0/167/110, gate 29s -> 98s. Variant exploration sets `repair_passes: 0` - ranking candidates that are about to be discarded should not cost 3x.
- **The planned repair optimisation is dead, and the measurement is why.** The idea was that repair re-routes the whole board to move a handful of nets, so ripping up only the nets named in violations would turn 23s into about a second. Measured instead of assumed - comparing every net's routed geometry before and after a repair pass:

| fixture | nets | changed by repair | first pass | with repair |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| stm32_breakout | 34 | **34 (100%)** | 6.6s | 27.8s |
| multi_ic | 47 | **45 (96%)** | 24.3s | 74.8s |

  Nothing to save. PathFinder is a negotiated-congestion router: blocking a few cells perturbs the congestion map that every net is planned against, so the whole board re-plans whether or not that was asked for. An incremental repair would either produce a different answer from the one the gate measures, or not be incremental.
- **What the numbers do suggest**, recorded rather than built: repair is already "route the board several times under different constraints and keep the best", which is the shape `variant.rs` implements for a different purpose. If the 3x wall clock ever needs paying down, unifying the two is the lever - not per-net rip-up.
- DONE: **the resolution question is settled, and the answer is that the current default is right.** Measured with `tests/resolution_sweep.rs` (`--ignored`), routing each fixture at its adaptive default and at finer grids, violations introduced by the router and seconds:

| fixture | 0.508mm | 0.254mm | 0.127mm |
|---|---|---|---|
| led_blink | - | 1 (0.3s) | 0 (1.1s) |
| stm32_breakout | - | 174 (20.3s) | 331 (42.2s) |
| multi_ic | 79 (73.2s) | 151 (135.8s) | skipped, 495023 cells past the 400000 cap |

  Halving the cell roughly doubles both the violation count and the runtime on the dense boards. That is not a paradox: the default 0.254mm is exactly `min_trace_width + min_clearance`, the legal track pitch, so neighbouring cells are clearance-legal by construction and a finer grid puts two nets close enough to touch. A quarter of the pitch was tried and abandoned - stm32_breakout ran over nine minutes without finishing against 20.5s at the default, and was killed; the sweep now skips past a cell cap and says so rather than leaving a row quietly missing. Anyone proposing a finer grid for the remaining violations should read this table first.
- **`grid_resolution_nm` was not being honoured on large boards.** `resolve_adaptive_grid_resolution` doubles the pitch above 80mm, and it applied that to an explicit setting too, so a caller asking for 0.254mm on multi_ic silently got 0.508mm - which is how the first version of the table above compared the same grid against itself and reported identical numbers twice. An explicit resolution is an instruction now. The sweep also prints the resolution the router will actually use rather than the one requested.
- NEXT-ACTION: none in this vector that is worth its cost right now. The 3x buys 176 -> 167 and 127 -> 110 with no board made worse, which is a fair trade for an autorouter; the next real gain is a better cost model, which is a project rather than a fire.

## Domain model - what a tool of this kind has to represent

Gaps found by working on the code, each one verified rather than assumed. These
are not features; they are things a board cannot be described without, and every
one is why some check, export or router decision is currently guessing. Add to
this list whenever a piece of work runs into a missing concept.

| # | Missing | Evidence | What it blocks |
|---|---|---|---|
| M1 | ~~Per-net electrical constraints on the board model.~~ **Closed.** The router routes a net at the width it states; the checker enforces the clearance it states. | - | The *router* still builds one grid from one preset, so it does not give a strict net more room while routing - the checker catches it afterwards. |
| M2 | ~~A net on a copper zone.~~ **Closed.** `Zone.net`, filled by sync, and the ratsnest drops a pad the pour already connects. | - | Filling a pour with real geometry and checking it against foreign copper are still open - see V5. |
| M3 | ~~Which side a component is on.~~ **Closed.** A `Side` component, set from `(layer "B.Cu")` on import and derived from the footprint's copper by sync, which the silkscreen rule now reads instead of guessing. | - | The DSL still has no word for it - a hand-written board is top-side. Grammar work, which needs `parser.c` regenerated. |
| M4 | ~~A board outline that is not a rectangle.~~ **Closed.** `outline { point ... }` in the DSL, assembled from Edge.Cuts on import, measured by edge clearance, and routed to by the Edge_Cuts gerber. | - | Nothing renders it yet - the viewer still draws the rectangle. |
| M5 | ~~Net classes.~~ **Closed.** `netclass Power [width 0.5mm] { VCC GND }` states a rule once for a group; a net's own block overrides it field by field. | - | - |
| M6 | ~~Silkscreen artwork per footprint.~~ **Closed.** Carried by `Footprint`, written in the DSL, accepted from a supplier, measured by the checker and printed by the gerber. | - | The viewer cannot call `register_footprint` - see the note under V4. |

That premise has moved: PathFinder now completes led_blink with 0 unrouted, 45 segments and 3 DRC violations, against 5 unrouted and a board it gave up on. Worth a look before the next release. |

Route development AROUND these. Never wait on them.

## Background jobs

**A log file is a snapshot too.** `/config/bench/full-test.log` was last written 2026-08-05 and ends with `error: 2 targets failed`, which reads as a red suite until you check its date. Both were re-run on HEAD on 2026-08-07 and both pass: `benchmark_validation` 1 passed 2 ignored, `smoother_integration` 1 passed. The failure it records is real history - `DRC violations should be ≤ 5 (S03 baseline), got 13` - and was fixed by the same-net exemption work on 2026-08-06, which is why the test now asserts `<= 2` and measures 2. The same board also routes in 0.08s where that log took 94.39s. Read the timestamp before reading the verdict.

Check live, do not trust any snapshot in this file:

```
docker exec -u abc code-server bash -lc 'export PATH=/config/.cargo/bin:$PATH; cd /workspace/codeyourpcb && cargo test --workspace --exclude cypcb-desktop -j 12 2>&1 | tail -5'
```

## Cadence

- Never idle. Finish an item, take the next unblocked NEXT-ACTION.
- Commit and push after every material step; move DONE/NEXT-ACTION in the same commit.
- Verify before marking done: a command whose output proves the claim goes in the commit message or this file.
- **`cargo test` and `cargo clippy` passing is not the gate passing.** Run `cargo fmt --check` before every commit - it is stage 1, it costs a second, and skipping it kept the gate red across six fires while every one of them reported green. `./scripts/quality-gate.sh` is the authority; run it whenever a fire touches the build, the viewer bundle or a script.
